The practical implication for fleet design is that peak TFLOP/s specifications are precision-dependent. The H100 delivers 989 TFLOP/s in FP16 but twice that (1,979 TFLOP/s) in FP8. FP8 therefore doubles the advertised arithmetic ceiling for supported operations on the same hardware. The complete training run gains less when communication, memory traffic, or higher-precision operations remain on the critical path. Precision engineering is therefore a primary optimization lever for infrastructure planners, alongside its effect on model accuracy.

Approaching peak throughput requires pipeline balance. Operand staging and reuse must supply the arithmetic units at the rate the operation requires. Kernel fusion and tiled attention such as FlashAttention improve that balance by keeping intermediate values on chip instead of making separate HBM round-trips, mechanisms that Kernel-Level Levers collects for reference. For our 175B model, the fleet-level gain depends on how much step time the affected kernels consume. A faster attention kernel shortens the step only in proportion to attention’s share of it, the same Amdahl accounting that bounds every partial speedup.

2.4.1 Peak vs. sustained throughput

A training run budgeted for 1,000 GPU-hours from datasheet TFLOP/s will consume 2,500 GPU-hours in practice when the workload sustains only 40 percent of peak throughput. Infrastructure planning therefore separates peak throughput (the maximum rate for the vendor-specified operation and precision) from sustained throughput (what the complete workload delivers). Peak TFLOP/s assumes that eligible arithmetic units remain fed under the stated sparsity, precision, and clock conditions; it does not include memory stalls, ineligible operations, communication, or software overhead. Sustained fractions therefore vary with model shape, batch size, precision, implementation, and scale. The gap arises from several sources, each representing a different physical or software limitation.

Memory stalls occur when the Tensor Cores complete their current tile multiplication before the next tile has been loaded from HBM. Even with the H100’s 3.35 TB/s HBM bandwidth, the Tensor Cores can consume data faster than HBM can deliver it for certain matrix dimensions, creating idle cycles while the arithmetic units wait for data.

Dependency stalls arise when one operation must complete before the next can begin, leaving some processing elements idle. In a Transformer layer, the attention computation must complete before the feed-forward network can begin, and the feed-forward computation must complete before the next layer’s attention can start. These sequential dependencies create brief periods where some hardware resources are unused, even within one accelerator. When the model is split into sequential stages on different accelerators (pipeline parallelism), the same dependencies become pipeline bubbles, idle slots in which a whole stage waits for its predecessor. Splitting each batch into microbatches reduces the waste by feeding later microbatches into stages that would otherwise wait. The term pipeline bubble refers only to this cross-device case, whose scheduling Distributed Training develops. At the infrastructure level, the lesson is that peak throughput assumes every unit stays busy, while real training includes dependency gaps that lower sustained utilization.

AllReduce operations used in tensor parallelism and gradient synchronization used in data parallelism can likewise delay computations that depend on their results; even when communication overlaps computation on separate streams, operations that depend on the collective output cannot proceed until the transfer completes. Kernel submission, memory allocation, and framework control also consume time, and when each kernel completes in microseconds, launch overhead of a similar size becomes a comparable share of the step that only a host-and-device execution trace can separate from slow arithmetic or memory access. Achieving high sustained utilization is therefore largely a matter of minimizing the time Tensor Cores spend idle between operations, which is why kernel fusion and specialized kernel libraries such as FlashAttention and the Transformer Engine exist.

The last source of the gap is the device itself, and no kernel change removes it. Device variation, sometimes called the silicon lottery, means that nominally identical accelerators sustain different clocks. Clock behavior also depends on the configured power limit, temperature, and workload, so a slow participant should not be diagnosed from its product label or frequency alone. In synchronous training, a participant that finishes dependent work late delays its whole process group. One device sustaining a clock about 2.5 percent below its peers can slow every synchronous step it joins by up to that fraction, because the other participants wait for it at each collective. Fleet management systems therefore track per-device sustained throughput and can move persistent underperformers to pools, such as inference serving, where they do not gate a synchronized group. Profiling must determine whether local arithmetic, memory, communication, or a power limit caused that delay before changing placement or replacing hardware.

Capacity planning must use a measured or conservatively modeled sustained rate, not the peak specification. A run estimated at 1,000 GPU-hours from peak FLOP/s requires \(1/\EfficiencyHw\) times that estimate when its measured utilization fraction is \(\EfficiencyHw\). Infrastructure teams track model FLOPs utilization (MFU), the useful model computation per second divided by the selected hardware peak, as the standard measure of \(\EfficiencyHw\), but comparisons remain meaningful only when model-FLOP conventions, precision, hardware peak, and system scale match.

↪ MFU as the measure of program goodput, one factor of fleet-level ML goodput, is developed in Program goodput and its measure.

The accelerator’s physics establish a concrete problem. Our 175B model requires 350 GB of memory for its weights alone in FP16, and Adam-style training state raises the requirement to multiple terabytes once optimizer moments, gradients, and activations are included. A single H100 provides 80 GB of HBM. No single accelerator can hold this model, an instance of the memory capacity gap (principle 2), in which frontier memory requirements outgrow single-accelerator HBM. The system must expand to the next physical level: the node.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An H100 advertises twice the dense arithmetic rate in FP8 that it advertises in FP16. A team sizes a training run’s GPU-hours by assuming the full FP8 rate. Which statement best describes the planning error?

    1. FP8 halves the bytes per weight, so memory-bound phases also run exactly twice as fast and the plan is correct.
    2. The FP8 rating is a ceiling for eligible matrix operations; ineligible operations, memory stalls, communication, and the need to validate the precision policy keep the sustained rate below it, so the plan must use a measured sustained rate.
    3. FP8 Tensor Core rates apply only to inference, so a training plan must use the FP32 rate instead.
    4. FP8 runs slower than FP16 on Tensor Cores because of scaling overhead, so the plan overestimates the time.

  2. A training run is estimated at 1,000 GPU-hours from peak FLOP/s. Profiling a representative window shows a model FLOPs utilization (MFU) of 0.4. How many GPU-hours should the plan budget, and what must match before this MFU can be compared with another team’s figure?

  3. True or False: In synchronous training, an accelerator that sustains a clock about 2.5 percent below its peers costs the job at most 2.5 percent of that one device’s throughput.

  4. The standard mixed-precision recipe runs the forward and backward passes in BF16 or FP16 but keeps a full-precision FP32 copy of the parameters, called the ____ weights, so that updates much smaller than a weight are not lost to FP16 rounding.

See Answers →

2.5 The Node

The running model needs more memory than one accelerator provides. A node supplies several accelerators in one chassis, but each retains its own memory; dividing the work creates communication that the local fabric must sustain. Tensor parallelism divides an operation across accelerators, pipeline parallelism assigns successive model stages to different groups, and data parallelism assigns different training examples to model replicas whose updates must be combined. These strategies impose different traffic patterns, and the node’s internal fabric decides which of them it can carry at the speed the partition needs.

Aggregating accelerators solves the capacity problem only if the interconnect between them is fast enough that the group behaves as one machine rather than as several machines passing messages. The 175B model’s weights span at least five accelerators, and the full training state spans an entire 8-H100 node and more, so some model state must live on different chips. Depending on the partition, execution exchanges partial results, activations, gradients, or parameter shards. Whether those exchanges are cheap or ruinous depends on the local fabric, which is why the node is the first scale level where the accelerator’s specifications matter less than the wires between accelerators. The training system can combine tensor parallelism, optimizer sharding, and memory offload as needed to meet its capacity budget, and each strategy places a different demand on the interconnect.

Definition 2.4: Node

Node is the ML training cluster’s physical server chassis that aggregates multiple accelerators (typically 8) through a high-speed intra-node interconnect (NVLink or ICI), providing a physical packaging and failure boundary. The fast communication domain can coincide with one chassis or extend across several, depending on the fabric.

  1. Significance: The NVLink bandwidth within a DGX H100 node reaches 900 GB/s bidirectional, or 450 GB/s per direction, while inter-node InfiniBand NDR provides 50 GB/s, a 9× per-direction gap that constrains where each parallelism strategy can run. For this DGX H100 topology, frequent tensor-parallel collectives favor the intra-node NVLink domain; pipeline parallelism can span nodes when the network sustains stage-boundary activation traffic. The node is also the primary failure domain: a single node failure in a bulk synchronous parallel training job, where workers advance in lockstep at step barriers, idles the entire cluster until recovery.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a single accelerator (which provides fast HBM bandwidth but limited capacity), a node aggregates 8\(\times\) the HBM capacity and 8\(\times\) the compute of a single chip. That is enough to place large FP16 weight shards (8 \(\times\) 80 GB = 640 GB on DGX H100), but not enough to keep the full Adam training state in HBM.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that nodes in a distributed training job operate independently. In bulk synchronous parallel training, all nodes are synchronously coupled at every step barrier: a performance degradation on one node (from thermal throttling, a faulty NIC, or a slow storage read) stalls the entire cluster and drops overall MFU for the duration of that step.

Why not hold the overflow in host DRAM instead of wiring eight accelerators together? A server’s DDR5 channels supply ample capacity (512 GB to 2 TB per node) at a fraction of HBM’s cost, but their bandwidth is orders of magnitude too slow to feed Tensor Cores on the active forward and backward path. Pooling the HBM of eight accelerators inside one chassis keeps the active working set in high-bandwidth memory, with the crucial caveat that each accelerator’s HBM remains private to its own package. Software must shard or replicate tensors explicitly and invoke collectives or peer-to-peer transfers whenever an operation crosses device boundaries, so an eight-GPU node delivers 8\(\times\) aggregate capacity and 8\(\times\) aggregate compute without ever behaving like a single shared-memory processor.

The capacity arithmetic also explains the appeal of multiple packages. Holding a 175B model’s 350 GB of FP16 weights on one accelerator, at the example 17–24 GB per HBM stack, would require 350 GB spread across roughly 15–21 stacks. That exceeds the 5-stack H100 and 8-stack B200-class configurations used here. Such a package would need its own area, yield, power, and cooling analysis. Distributing capacity across 8 accelerators instead uses available packages and pays for communication between their private memories.

2.5.1 Bandwidth hierarchy

Placing a layer-by-layer tensor-parallel collective across a 50 GB/s inter-node link instead of a 450 GB/s intra-node link multiplies its transfer floor ninefold and stalls every accelerator in the group. Multi-accelerator systems are governed less by the number of chips than by the speed at which they can exchange data across physical boundaries. Data movement speed drops by orders of magnitude as it crosses package, board, and rack boundaries, each with a different physical medium, connector technology, and signaling constraint. This physical ordering of data transfer rates, from on-chip SRAM at the fast end to the wide-area network at the slow end, is the bandwidth hierarchy; this ordering dictates where in the hierarchy each type of parallelism can operate efficiently.

The hierarchy reflects the physical cost of distance. Shorter signal paths over denser wiring achieve higher throughput at lower energy per bit, so at each boundary the effective bandwidth \((\Bandwidth)\) drops by roughly an order of magnitude while latency \((\Latency)\) rises. The exact size of each gap depends on link count, signaling, and the measurement boundary, but the gradient itself sets a scaling ceiling for distributed training. The common mistake is to treat all cluster communication as equal. Parallelism placement must be hierarchy-aware, because putting high-frequency synchronization such as tensor parallelism on a slow tier leaves the arithmetic units \((\PeakRate)\) waiting and collapses scaling efficiency \((\ScalingEfficiency)\).

The bandwidth hierarchy can be read as concentric zones around each accelerator (figure 9), losing 7.4\(\times\) to 9\(\times\) of bandwidth at each physical boundary on the way out through NVLink, InfiniBand, and remote storage. The innermost zone is the HBM on the same package, with terabytes per second of bandwidth and sub-microsecond access latency. The next zone holds the other accelerators in the node’s NVLink domain, reachable at hundreds of gigabytes per second per GPU with microsecond-scale transfer latency. The outermost zone spans the other nodes in the cluster, reached through InfiniBand ports that each supply tens of gigabytes per second per direction, with point-to-point latency measured in single-digit microseconds. Latency figures are comparable only when they measure the same thing (a memory access, a link transfer, or a complete collective), and bandwidth ratios only when they compare matching endpoints and directions, as the representative paths of table 9 do.

Napkin Math 2.3: The physics of the staircase
Problem: A 10 GB buffer must move one hop at each tier of the hierarchy, with a local HBM read as the reference point. How much does the tier it crosses change the transfer time?

Math: Transfer time is \(\TransferTimeForPayload= \DataVolume / \Bandwidth\).

  1. HBM (local read, the reference point): 10 GB / 3,350 GB/s \(\approx\) 3.0 ms.
  2. NVLink (intra-node): 10 GB / 450 GB/s \(\approx\) 22.2 ms.
  3. InfiniBand (inter-node): 10 GB / 50 GB/s \(\approx\) 200 ms.

Systems insight: For the same payload, the example inter-node path has a transfer-time lower bound 9× larger than the example intra-node path. Size alone does not decide which traffic may cross that cliff; frequency and overlap do. Tensor parallelism exchanges activations inside every layer, on the critical path, so it favors the fast local NVLink domain in this configuration. Data parallelism moves the largest buffer, the full gradient, but only once per step and overlapped with the backward pass, so it can cross the scarce InfiniBand tier. A partition that fits in memory can still spend much of a step waiting if its exposed exchanges exceed the communication budget.

Each cliff in the bandwidth staircase has a physical cause at its distance scale. Millimeter-long traces on a silicon interposer can carry a wide, slow-clocked parallel interface without serializer/deserializer (SerDes) transceivers. NVLink crosses tens of centimeters of board copper, which requires SerDes transceivers with equalization to recover the signal at high per-lane rates. PCIe pays for universal compatibility with protocol overhead (packet headers, flow-control credits, and error checking). InfiniBand crosses meters of cable between racks, adding optical conversion on longer runs, forward error correction, and switch hops. SerDes, link budget, and power develops the signaling physics behind these cliffs. For node design, link count and sustained bandwidth must be evaluated together with the power and cooling load of the components that provide them.

The practical consequence for model partitioning is that tensor parallelism (TP), which splits individual matrix multiplications across accelerators and requires collective exchanges within layers, generates communication volume proportional to the model’s hidden dimension hundreds of times per second. Over NVLink, each of these exchanges takes under a millisecond for the running model (section 2.5.2 computes the idealized lower bound). At the reference InfiniBand port rate, the bandwidth-only lower bound for the same exchange is 9× as large; the step-time effect also depends on startup, routing, and overlap. For this topology TP therefore stays within a node, although a system whose fast scale-up fabric spans several chassis can place a tensor-parallel group across them; communication cost, rather than the server enclosure, determines the useful partition.

The hierarchy also explains why pipeline parallelism occupies an intermediate position in the bandwidth requirements. In pipeline parallelism, the model is divided into sequential stages, with each stage assigned to a different group of accelerators. The communication between stages consists of activations flowing forward during the forward pass and gradients flowing backward during the backward pass, with a volume proportional to the batch size times the hidden dimension.

For our 175B model with hidden dimension 12,288 and a microbatch of 4 sequences at 2,048, the activation tensor at each stage boundary is approximately \(4 \times 2,048 \times 12,288 \times 2 B\) bytes (FP16) \(\approx\) 201 MB. This is far smaller than the 350 GB gradient AllReduce required by data parallelism, which is why pipeline parallelism places less demanding requirements on the inter-node network.

Activation transfers occur once per microbatch per stage boundary. That is far less often than tensor parallelism’s AllReduce inside every layer, yet more often than data parallelism’s gradient exchange, which happens once per step. Small messages at an intermediate frequency let pipeline parallelism tolerate the lower bandwidth of inter-node links, which makes it the natural way to split a model whose layers do not fit in one node.

Both pipeline and data parallelism therefore cross node boundaries, for different reasons (table 10). Pipeline traffic is small per message and sits between the other two in frequency. The data-parallel gradient exchange is the largest transfer, but it occurs once per step and can overlap the backward pass. That overlap makes it the most latency-tolerant of the three, although any unfinished exchange still delays the update. For the example topology, the resulting mapping places tensor parallelism within the node, pipeline stages across nodes, and data-parallel groups across the cluster. This de facto pattern is a starting point for the capacity and timing calculation; a different scale-up domain, message size, or overlap opportunity can justify a different placement.

The mapping constrains how the scheduler assigns devices and nodes. Members of a tensor-parallel group belong inside one fast domain (on this topology, one chassis joined by NVLink), because their dependent exchanges recur inside every layer. Adjacent pipeline stages belong in the same rack or neighboring racks, where their activation and gradient traffic crosses the fewest InfiniBand switch hops. Data-parallel groups can span the whole cluster. Physical adjacency helps only when it corresponds to better connectivity. The node’s PCIe hierarchy adds another placement constraint by connecting accelerators to host CPUs, network interface cards (NICs), and storage, including the path used to reach host DRAM.

The PCIe hierarchy within a node

While NVLink carries GPU-to-GPU traffic, PCIe Gen5 connects accelerators, host CPUs, network adapters, and storage through the node’s peripheral hierarchy. Host DRAM attaches to the CPU’s memory controllers; a GPU transfer to that memory also traverses its host-facing PCIe path. PCIe bandwidth, approximately 64 GB/s per direction (128 GB/s bidirectional) per x16 link, can therefore constrain data loading, checkpointing, or GPU-to-network transfers. Which traffic competes depends on the actual links and switches those paths share.

A standard DGX H100 node features two host CPUs, each managing a PCIe root complex, and PCIe switches fan those lanes out. Each of the 8 GPUs and each of the 8 InfiniBand host channel adapters (HCAs, the network interface cards of an InfiniBand fabric) has its own x16 link (figure 10). This configuration provides an aggregate theoretical bandwidth of about 2 TB/s within the chassis. However, this bandwidth is shared among competing traffic streams. During a single training step, the PCIe bus simultaneously carries host-to-GPU data batches, GPU-to-HCA gradient shards for inter-node AllReduce (via GPUDirect remote direct memory access (RDMA)), and periodic CPU-to-NVMe checkpoint writes. Contention between these streams can cause unexpected pipeline stalls, particularly when gradient synchronization saturates the links typically used for data loading.

The dual-CPU architecture introduces significant non-uniform memory access (NUMA) effects. The 8 GPUs are physically partitioned, with 4 connected to CPU 0’s root complex and 4 to CPU 1’s. A GPU connected to CPU 0 can reach CPU 0’s DRAM through its local PCIe root complex, whereas access to CPU 1’s DRAM also traverses the inter-socket link. The latency and bandwidth penalty depends on the processor, socket interconnect, PCIe topology, transfer size, and concurrent traffic, so no fixed percentage applies across nodes. Data-loading pipelines should measure this topology and place worker processes, pinned buffers, NICs, and target GPUs within the same NUMA domain when the local path improves sustained ingestion. Placement is therefore part of the parallelism strategy, because a scheduler that ignores PCIe roots, NIC locality, and NUMA domains can turn an otherwise valid tensor- or pipeline-parallel plan into a host-bound bottleneck.

↪ Mapping data, tensor, and pipeline parallelism onto intra-node and inter-node bandwidth tiers is developed in Hybrid Parallelism.

2.5.2 Dense node designs

Given the bandwidth hierarchy, the node must connect 8 accelerators with enough bandwidth for tensor-parallel operations across their private memories. A switched fabric can provide full bisection bandwidth, enough capacity across any equal division of the endpoints to carry their full injection rate. Comparing a ring, a mesh, and a switched design shows what connectivity costs and which communication patterns it supports.

Eight GPUs in one chassis can be wired as a ring, a full mesh, or a switched fabric (figure 11). A ring is inexpensive, needing only \(\AcceleratorsPerNode\) links for \(\AcceleratorsPerNode\) GPUs, and suits ring-AllReduce, in which each GPU sends to one neighbor while receiving from the other so that every link stays busy. Tensor parallelism, however, issues frequent collectives after sharded matrix operations. These include ReduceScatter, which leaves each participant with one shard of a combined result, and AllGather, which assembles shards at every participant. A ring’s fixed multi-hop paths (up to four hops between distant GPUs) constrain those patterns. A full mesh reaches every peer in one hop but needs \(\AcceleratorsPerNode(\AcceleratorsPerNode-1) / 2 = 28\) links for 8 GPUs, a count that grows quadratically and soon becomes impractical to route. The NVSwitch crossbar puts every pair one switch hop apart and provides full-bisection switched connectivity with a manageable number of switch chips.

Consider the NVIDIA DGX H100 architecture. Eight H100 GPUs sit on a single baseboard, each connected to four NVSwitch chips via 18 NVLink 4.0 lanes. The NVSwitch chips form a non-blocking crossbar5 through which any GPU can reach any other GPU. Each GPU’s port budget is 900 GB/s bidirectional, or 450 GB/s in one direction, shared across that GPU’s simultaneous transfers; it is not a separate full-bandwidth link for every GPU pair.

5 Crossbar switch: A topology providing a dedicated path between every input-output pair simultaneously. An \(\SwitchRadix{\times}\SwitchRadix\) crossbar needs \(\SwitchRadix^2\) crosspoints, so its cost grows quadratically with port count; that growth is why a full crossbar stays inside a node and larger systems switch to multi-stage fabrics. Within that bound, the crossbar buys the all-to-all bandwidth that tensor parallelism demands.

The result is a node with 640 GB of aggregate HBM capacity and a combined peak compute throughput of 8 \(\times\) 1,979 TFLOP/s, while each GPU retains private HBM. Training frameworks partition tensors explicitly across those devices, and NVSwitch reduces the communication cost between shards without making placement or collective synchronization transparent. The NVSwitch chips themselves consume approximately 200 W each (800 W total for the NVSwitch fabric), a nontrivial power overhead justified by the bisection bandwidth it provides. In physical terms, the NVSwitch fabric alone consumes more power than an entire previous-generation GPU (the V100 drew 300 W).

Tracing one tensor-parallel step of the running model shows what that NVSwitch power budget buys. Assume a schedule with one activation-sized AllReduce per layer in each forward and backward pass; the partition and collective schedule are scenario inputs, not a universal Transformer schedule. During the modeled tensor-parallel forward pass, each GPU holds one shard of each weight matrix and computes its portion of the matrix multiplication. For a Transformer layer with hidden dimension 12,288 (typical for a 175B model), each of the 8 GPUs holds a \(12{,}288{\times}1{,}536\) slice of the weight matrix at the projection that reduces the split inner dimension back to the full hidden width. Because that slice keeps the full output width while covering only one eighth of the inner sum, each GPU produces a full-width vector of partial sums for every token, and an AllReduce operation sums those partial results across all 8 GPUs over NVLink.

The logical output tensor for one AllReduce contains \(4 \times 2{,}048 \times 12{,}288 \times 2\) bytes (FP16). For a microbatch of 4 sequences at 2,048 tokens, that payload is roughly 201 MB. An ideal ring AllReduce with 8 participants moves \(2(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount=\) 1.75 times the payload per GPU (Collective Communication derives this factor), or about 352 MB. At 450 GB/s of one-direction bandwidth, the transfer-time lower bound is approximately 0.783 ms.

Across 96 forward layers and 96 backward layers, the 192 transfers sum to about 150.3 ms per step before overlap. NVSwitch provides the paths needed for the collective, but this value remains an ideal bandwidth-only lower bound that excludes startup latency, software overhead, and competing traffic.

↪ The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model, which adds the fixed per-message startup latency this bound omits to the bytes-over-bandwidth transfer time, is developed in The \MessageStartupLatency-\LinkBandwidth Communication Model.

Judging that bound needs a compute time to compare it with. This scenario assumes 200 ms of computation per step for the tensor-parallel group, a stated input rather than a derived value; the fractions below scale with it, so a longer compute time makes the same communication matter less. Against that assumption, the unoverlapped NVLink lower bound leaves computation at 57.1 percent of combined compute-plus-communication time. Actual tensor-parallel efficiency can be higher when collectives overlap useful computation or lower when latency, contention, load imbalance, and nonparallel work add delay, so it must be measured rather than inferred from peak link bandwidth alone.

Example 2.3: The NVLink bandwidth surprise
Scenario: A team evaluates tensor parallelism over a hypothetical PCIe Gen5 path with 64 GB/s bandwidth, using the payload and the assumed 200 ms per-step compute time stated with the NVLink estimate above.

Diagnosis: The modeled tensor-parallel schedule executes 192 AllReduce events per step across 96 layers. Its PCIe bandwidth-only transfer times sum to 1057 ms per step. Without overlap, communication accounts for 84.1 percent of the combined compute and transfer estimate; this is not a measured stall fraction.

Systems lesson: Under the same assumptions, NVLink lowers the transfer bound to 0.783 ms per AllReduce and raises computation to 57.1 percent of the combined time. The comparison explains why this partition favors a fast local fabric. Real collective performance must also include startup, topology, contention, and overlap.

The DGX H100 pairs each GPU with its own InfiniBand ConnectX-7 HCA, which reads and writes GPU memory directly over PCIe through GPUDirect RDMA, so inter-node gradient exchange avoids staging copies in host DRAM while still consuming PCIe and network bandwidth. The host CPU prepares and loads batches, submits kernels, and coordinates the training framework. Sufficient CPU capacity and I/O bandwidth let that work overlap the accelerator fabric’s computation and communication; otherwise, slow preprocessing or submission can starve the GPUs even when their local memory and interconnect are fast enough.

↪ The RDMA and GPUDirect data path that bypasses host memory is developed in RDMA and GPUDirect.

Kanev, Svilen, Juan Pablo Darago, Kim Hazelwood, Parthasarathy Ranganathan, Tipp Moseley, Gu-Yeon Wei, and David Brooks. 2015. “Profiling a Warehouse-Scale Computer.” Proceedings of the 42nd Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture, 158–69. https://doi.org/10.1145/2749469.2750392.

Host CPUs also pay a “data center tax” of shared infrastructure work. A profile of Google’s fleet found that such common building blocks consumed nearly 30 percent of cycles across jobs (Kanev et al. 2015), and network protocol processing, storage virtualization, and security functions add to that load on cloud hosts. ML nodes can therefore incorporate data processing units (DPUs) or SmartNICs. Devices like the NVIDIA BlueField DPU offload these infrastructure tasks to dedicated ARM cores and hardware accelerators integrated into the network adapter itself. Moving the control plane for RDMA, firewall rules, and storage protocols to the DPU recovers cycles for the ML pipeline: data loading, tokenization, and kernel orchestration on the host CPU. The DPU also acts as an isolated security domain, allowing cloud providers to maintain control over the network and storage layer via the DPU while granting customers full access to the host CPU and GPUs. For our 175B model training, DPU offload can keep RDMA protocol processing outside the host CPU critical path for gradient synchronization, which flows from GPU HBM toward the network fabric without host CPU involvement.

Alternative node architectures

Spending board area, power, and cost on four NVSwitch chips makes sense when tensor-parallel collectives sit inside every layer, but workloads dominated by embedding capacity, data parallelism, or cost-sensitive serving hit different limits first. Alternative node designs reallocate that budget. An AMD MI300X server emphasizes large HBM capacity on each package, Google TPU pods wire chips directly into a mesh without a central baseboard switch, and Intel/Habana Gaudi nodes integrate Ethernet RDMA ports directly onto the accelerator die. Comparing them against a DGX H100 node reveals where each architecture spends its scarce budget.

In a switch-rich node like the DGX H100, universal any-to-any local exchange is the priority because each Transformer layer places intra-node collectives inside the model’s inner loop. The 900 GB/s H100 NVLink fabric therefore buys a software abstraction, in which the training framework can treat eight accelerators as one tightly coupled tensor-parallel group for the layers that must be split across chips.

Other systems move that boundary. TPU pods use the inter-chip interconnect and a mesh-oriented topology instead of making a central switch the defining component of the node; a TPU v5p chip’s vendor-specified ICI bandwidth is 1,200 GB/s. The mesh can reduce switch cost and expose a regular structure to the compiler, but it also makes placement more visible, because the runtime must respect neighbor relationships rather than assuming that every chip pair has the same communication path. That is a reasonable trade when the workload is regular enough for the compiler and collective libraries to map communication onto the topology.

The MI300X shifts the pressure point from local switching toward memory capacity. Its package exposes 192 GB of HBM at 5.3 TB/s, compared with 80 GB on an H100-class accelerator. An eight-accelerator MI300X node would therefore provide 1,536 GB of aggregate HBM, enough to hold the 350 GB FP16 weight tensor for our 175B running model. That does not eliminate the optimizer, gradient, activation, and fragmentation problem that section 2.5.3 quantifies, but it changes how often the system must spill state into slower memory tiers and how much microbatch headroom remains before offload becomes mandatory.

Gaudi takes a different route by integrating RDMA-capable Ethernet into the accelerator and using a more network-centric design for communication. A Gaudi 2 accelerator’s 24 integrated 100 GbE ports supply 300 GB/s of aggregate RDMA over Converged Ethernet (RoCE) bandwidth, against the 900 GB/s of local exchange per GPU in an H100-class NVLink node. The newer Gaudi 3 keeps that Ethernet-centric design while carrying 128 GB of HBM per accelerator, so capacity is not the trade being made. The question is whether simplifying the fabric and using Ethernet consistently across node and pod is worth giving up the high local exchange bandwidth of a switch-rich NVLink design. That trade can be attractive when data parallelism, inference serving, or cluster cost dominates, and less attractive when tensor-parallel collectives sit inside every layer.

These alternatives are not a ranking of vendors. They are different placements of scarce resources: switching bandwidth in the node, regular topology in the pod, HBM capacity in the package, or network attachment on the accelerator. For the 175B model used throughout this chapter, the tensor-parallel portion of training makes local accelerator communication unusually important, so the DGX-style answer is natural. In other regimes, especially memory-heavy recommenders, topology-regular training, or cost-sensitive serving fleets, a different boundary can become the one that matters most. Software ecosystem maturity, supply availability, and total cost of ownership then decide whether the theoretical architectural fit becomes a deployable system.

2.5.3 Node memory partitioning

The node’s aggregate HBM must hold far more than the weights, and how that state divides across the node’s memory tiers decides which parallelism strategies are possible. Consider the memory budget for training our 175B model. The model weights in FP16 require 350 GB. The Adam optimizer maintains two additional values for every parameter (first and second moments) in FP32 precision, adding 175 \(\times 10^9 \times 2 \times 4\) bytes = 1,400 GB. Gradients require another 350 GB. This subtotal is approximately 2.1 TB, far exceeding both a single accelerator’s 80 GB HBM and an 8-H100 node’s 640 GB aggregate HBM, and it still omits two terms. Activations are the intermediate results the backward pass needs. The FP32 master weights are a full-precision copy of the parameters that the optimizer updates, kept because an update far smaller than a weight would be lost to FP16 rounding; this is the mixed-precision copy introduced with the precision formats in section 2.4.

A common misconception is that a model’s memory footprint equals its weight storage. With those two terms included, the FP32 master weights, optimizer moments, gradients, and activations collectively consume about 7–8\(\times\) more memory than the FP16 parameters alone. These components reside in different tiers of the node’s memory hierarchy. For our 175B model, training memory partitions across six distinct components (table 11).

The 2.9–3.2 TB budget of table 11 extends the earlier subtotal with the master weights and activations. Its five persistent rows sum to 16 bytes per parameter, or 2.8 TB, the training state given for Archetype A in Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3), and activations add the rest. The Adam optimizer state in FP32, not the FP16 weights, is the dominant term, and the activation peak, which scales with batch size and sequence length, is the most variable. The lesson for parallelism selection is that the FP16 weights are among the smaller pieces of what the node must hold, so a strategy that only shards weights leaves the largest consumers, the FP32 optimizer moments and master weights, untouched.

A DGX H100 node provides 640 GB of aggregate HBM across its 8 GPUs, plus 2 TB of host DDR5 DRAM. The HBM capacity alone is insufficient for the full training state, as the memory arithmetic for each parallelism strategy shows. With pure data parallelism, each GPU must hold replicated state, and even the 2,100 GB subtotal of weights, gradients, and optimizer moments, before master weights and activations, is physically impossible on an 80 GB device. Even ZeRO stage 3, when confined to a single node that shards all three components across only 8 GPUs, yields 2,100 GB / 8 ≈ 262.5 GB per GPU, still more than triple the available HBM.

Fitting the state into HBM therefore requires expanding across multiple nodes so that a larger pool of accelerators shares the memory footprint. The standard approach combines intra-node tensor parallelism (which splits tensors and operations within layers across GPUs, reducing per-GPU weights to 350 GB / 8 = 43.75 GB) with ZeRO-style optimizer sharding across the data-parallel replicas on other nodes. In a cluster configuration with TP-8 within each node and 128-way data parallelism across nodes, each node acts as one model replica whose GPUs keep their 43.75 GB weight shards resident for every forward and backward layer. Because the optimizer moments are needed only once per step during the parameter update, they do not need to stay replicated on every node and are partitioned twice, first by the 8-way tensor split and then across the 128 data-parallel replicas. Each GPU therefore holds approximately 1,400 GB / (1024) ≈ 1.4 GB of optimizer state, or 10.9 GB per 8-GPU tensor-parallel group, before gradients, activations, buffers, and fragmentation. The weight-plus-optimizer subtotal of roughly 45.1 GB is only the starting point. The memory-budget exercise in section 2.5.3.1 examines a single node without this cross-node sharding and shows why optimizer offload alone is insufficient there. This arithmetic is why tensor parallelism, optimizer sharding, activation checkpointing, and memory tiering are used together for large-model training. The node is therefore a high-bandwidth compute and communication unit, not a standalone container for the entire training state.

Because even full ZeRO-3 sharding within one node leaves each GPU above its HBM capacity, sharding must be combined with other techniques. Activation checkpointing stores only a subset of intermediate activations for the backward pass and recomputes the rest from the nearest checkpoint, trading extra forward computation for activation memory, a trade that Compute-phase forward and backward passes develops.

CPU offloading stores state in host DDR5 DRAM and may also move optimizer computation to the CPU. The implementation determines which gradients, weights, or optimizer buffers traverse the PCIe Gen5 path at 64 GB/s. Its cost depends on those bytes, host computation, and the time available to overlap transfers; a short GPU update does not establish cheap offload. NVMe offloading extends storage to SSDs, with the illustrative 5–7 GB/s sequential read rate introducing a further bandwidth constraint that prefetching can hide only when consumption leaves enough time.

An offloaded configuration uses the node’s memory hierarchy as a tiered system sorted by how often each piece of state is touched. HBM holds the weight shards, activations, and accumulating gradients needed on every layer of the forward and backward passes. Host DDR5 absorbs the optimizer moments and master weights, which are read and written only once per step during the parameter update. NVMe extends capacity for the largest models by holding seldom-accessed shards that can be prefetched within the step budget. A configuration with sufficient distributed HBM can instead retain optimizer shards on accelerators. The placement follows the capacity and transfer-time calculation, rather than a fixed assignment of every state type to one tier.

The training framework’s memory manager moves state between these tiers much as a cache controller does, prefetching the next layer’s offloaded weights into HBM during the current layer’s computation and evicting state that will not be needed soon. Prefetch depth sets the balance, because too shallow a prefetch stalls computation on PCIe transfers and too deep a prefetch crowds out the current step’s activations and gradients. Table 12 quantifies the gaps the manager must bridge, more than two orders of magnitude in bandwidth between HBM and an NVMe drive. For our 175B model, a capacity plan must therefore choose among additional sharding, offload, and reductions in live state, and the next budget tests whether a proposed combination actually fits within one node.

Node-level memory budget

Combining all five memory components for our 175B model on a single DGX H100 node in example 2.4 shows why closing the budget requires coordinating sharding, checkpointing, and offload together rather than tuning any one component in isolation.

Example 2.4: Memory budget for 175B training on DGX H100
Scenario: An engineering team calculates the memory budget for training a 175B parameter model using 8-way tensor parallelism on an 8-GPU DGX H100 node (80 GB HBM per GPU).

Diagnosis: Without ZeRO sharding, each GPU’s TP-8 slice of the optimizer state alone requires 175 GB per GPU, which is more than the weights and gradients combined (88 GB). A separate FP32 master-weight shard adds 87.5 GB. Together with retained FP16 gradients, the assumed activations, and workspace, these components bring the per-GPU budget to 375 GB, well past the 80 GB HBM limit. That budget already assumes activation checkpointing; its activation peak is a scenario input that must be replaced by measurements for the chosen microbatch and sequence length.

Systems lesson: Sizing training memory requires managing weights, gradients, optimizer states, activations, and buffers holistically, because relieving one component does not necessarily clear the envelope. Offloading the optimizer moments and master weights to host DDR5 removes those FP32 components and still leaves 113 GB per GPU, because 8-way tensor parallelism keeps a full weight and gradient shard resident on every GPU. Further reductions require offloading or shrinking another live component. Alternatively, adding devices creates an additional data-parallel group across which ZeRO can shard state. The same eight GPUs already assigned to TP-8 do not provide an independent eight-way data-parallel sharding axis; parallel degrees multiply to the allocated device count.

2.5.4 Data loading and I/O pipeline

Even after the node’s memory budget closes, its accelerators still need a continuous stream of training data from outside the chassis. If the data loading pipeline cannot keep pace with the accelerators’ consumption rate, the most expensive component in the node sits idle; this is the I/O bottleneck. For pre-tokenized language-model training, the raw demand is small: at 500,000 tokens/s stored as 4-byte token IDs, ingestion is only 2 MB/s. The pressure falls instead on the host CPU cores that read, decode, shuffle, and batch examples, on the PCIe paths that data shares with gradient and checkpoint traffic (section 2.5.1.1), and on shared storage that every node reads at once. GPUDirect Storage can remove the host-memory bounce copy for data that needs no host-side preprocessing, and staging data on local NVMe can absorb synchronized bursts that would otherwise hit a shared filesystem. For node design, the hardware contract is end-to-end sustained bandwidth at the intended concurrency: storage servers, network paths, host preprocessing, host memory, and accelerator transfer must all keep pace, because a local benchmark that satisfies one node says little about a shared path serving the fleet. Keeping that node productive over a multi-week run then turns on how often a component fault inside the chassis interrupts execution.

↪ Sizing the input pipeline end to end, with every stage from storage read to host-to-device transfer held at or above the required bandwidth, is developed in The Data Pipeline Equation.

2.5.5 Node health and reliability

When a single GPU suffers an uncorrectable HBM error or a rack power shelf trips, it does not fail in isolation; it takes its entire eight-GPU tensor-parallel group or multi-node rack out of the synchronous training step. A failure domain is the set of resources that a single fault takes out of service together, and the physical hierarchy nests these domains inside one another. A failed GPU breaks its tensor-parallel group, which usually spans its node. The node sits in a rack whose shared power shelf, top-of-rack switch, and cooling loop can take every node in it down at once. Racks share row-level power and coolant distribution, and halls share a building’s utility feed, cooling plant, and fire compartment. For a synchronous training job the node is the smallest domain that matters. An eight-way tensor-parallel group within a node becomes unusable when any one GPU fails, because its collectives require all eight participants. The node’s effective MTBF depends on the combined failure exposure of every component whose loss stops the job, as well as shared power and cooling failures. The node’s figure is therefore an MTBF, the expected interval between successive failures of a unit that keeps running through replacements, while each GPU contributes an MTTF, the distinction drawn with the reliability wall in section 2.1.

Failure exposure spans memory, interconnects, and the electrical and mechanical systems that support them. An uncorrectable error-correcting code (ECC) fault in an HBM stack forces the accelerator out of service and aborts the active training step. High-speed links degrade as repeated thermal cycles stress connectors and cables, which shows up as link retraining or a fatal communication drop. Power-supply components and the pumps and fans of the cooling path wear out under sustained load, and a loss of power or cooling can take several accelerators down together. The accelerator itself usually dominates the count. In Meta’s report on the Llama 3 405B pretraining run, faulty GPUs and HBM3 memory errors together caused nearly half of the unexpected interruptions, ahead of software bugs and network switches and cables (Dubey et al. 2024). The ordering in a given fleet still depends on its equipment and operating conditions, so local incident data should confirm it before it guides spare capacity or preventive maintenance.

Dubey, Abhimanyu, Abhinav Jauhri, Abhinav Pandey, Abhishek Kadian, et al. 2024. The Llama 3 Herd of Models. arXiv preprint arXiv:2407.21783.

Health monitoring can flag a node for investigation before another long job lands on it. A rising rate of corrected ECC errors, rising junction temperature at constant load, and intermittent link retraining are the most useful early signals, although no single signal establishes a cause or a time to failure. The fleet telemetry that collects them is developed in Fleet telemetry and hardware observability. For our 175B model training across 128 nodes, a composite node-level MTBF assumption of 1,000 hours, which includes GPUs, host components, power, cooling, and network links, predicts about 43 node failures over a run of 336 hours, roughly 3.1 failures/day. A GPU-only bound from the per-GPU MTTF of 50,000 hours would predict only 6.9. The node, not the GPU, is therefore the unit that spare capacity and recovery tooling must be sized for. The same accounting, applied to every component of a facility and then to every GPU of a fleet, reappears at larger scope in the facility reliability controls of the rack and the hours-scale fleet failure interval of section 2.7.1.

↪ Layered fault detection, from ECC and error-rate monitoring to known-answer stress tests on nodes pulled from production, is developed in Fleet-scale detection and mitigation.

For our 175B model, a single DGX H100 node provides 640 GB of aggregate HBM, enough to hold the FP16 model weights but not the full Adam training state without sharding and offload. Training also requires processing hundreds of billions of tokens, and a single node would need more than a year of compute to do so (section 2.7 works through the numbers), so completing training in weeks requires tens to hundreds of nodes. Stacking those nodes into a physical enclosure defines the next level of infrastructure, where the constraints shift from bandwidth and capacity to raw power and heat.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team considers running 8-way tensor parallelism across 8 separate single-GPU server nodes connected by InfiniBand NDR (\(50\text{ GB/s}\) per GPU), rather than within an 8-GPU DGX H100 node over NVSwitch (\(450\text{ GB/s}\) per direction per GPU). What is the primary system consequence on training step time?

    1. Tensor parallelism transfers only stage-boundary activations, so the network difference produces less than a 1% change in step time.
    2. InfiniBand protocols cannot transmit floating-point data, forcing CPU format conversions on every step.
    3. Inter-node tensor parallelism requires optical circuit switching, causing laser thermal shutdown.
    4. Tensor parallelism requires frequent layer-level exchanges; placing them on the \(\approx 9\times\) slower per-direction fabric increases exposed communication and can substantially reduce MFU.

  2. Rank the quoted peak data-movement bandwidth per GPU across the four primary hardware tiers in a modern DGX H100 cluster, from highest quoted bandwidth to lowest: (1) Inter-node InfiniBand NDR, (2) Intra-package HBM3, (3) Intra-node NVLink 4 (one direction), (4) Host-to-device PCIe Gen5 x16.

  3. A training framework offloads Adam first- and second-moment optimizer states (FP32, 8 bytes per parameter total) to host DDR5 DRAM over PCIe Gen5, while keeping weights and activations in GPU HBM. Justify this asymmetric placement using access frequency and bandwidth requirements, and quantify the HBM capacity freed for a 175B-parameter model.

  4. A Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) workload routes tokens dynamically across experts, producing heavy AllToAll collective traffic where every GPU in a node sends distinct token buffers to every other GPU. Why does an NVSwitch crossbar topology significantly outperform a physical GPU ring topology for this workload?

    1. NVSwitch provides non-blocking full bisection bandwidth enabling any-GPU-to-any-GPU communication in a single hop, whereas a ring forces non-neighbor transfers through intermediate hops, causing link contention and \(\mathcal{O}(N)\) latency.
    2. NVSwitch converts AllToAll network collectives into local register-file reads, eliminating inter-GPU data transfer entirely.
    3. Ring topologies cannot support mixed precision, forcing all token embeddings to be cast to FP64.
    4. NVSwitch increases individual GPU HBM3 bandwidth from \(3.35\text{ TB/s}\) to \(19\text{ TB/s}\).

  5. The supported direct storage-to-GPU data path that can remove the host-memory bounce copy for data requiring no host-side preprocessing is called ____.

  6. In a dual-socket server node hosting 8 GPUs across two CPU root complexes, explain how Non-Uniform Memory Access (NUMA) effects impact data loading throughput and what architectural scheduling rule prevents CPU-to-GPU bottlenecks.

See Answers →

2.6 The Rack

A standard 42U server rack in a traditional data center draws about 12 kW and can be cooled by room-temperature air pushed through perforated floor tiles. The chapter’s four-node DGX H100 rack scenario draws 33.5 kW, a figure that includes the rack’s allocated cooling overhead. At this density, the engineering constraints shift from silicon and signal integrity to power delivery and thermodynamics. The rack is where the power wall and heat transfer become dominant design forces.

For our 175B model, training across 1,024 GPUs requires 32 racks (4 nodes of 8 GPUs each per rack). Across the cluster, the modeled rack power, which includes each rack’s allocated cooling overhead, sums to approximately 1.1 MW. Delivering this power reliably and removing the resulting heat without allowing any component to exceed its thermal limit is a multidisciplinary engineering challenge spanning electrical, mechanical, and civil engineering. A failure at any point in the power delivery chain, from the utility substation to the individual GPU voltage regulator, can halt the entire training run, wasting hours of computation and potentially corrupting the training state.

Definition 2.5: Rack

Rack is the physical infrastructure unit, a standardized 42U enclosure, that houses multiple compute nodes, a top-of-rack (ToR) switch (the first network aggregation point connecting all nodes in the rack to the broader cluster fabric), power distribution units, and cooling distribution manifolds, defining the granularity at which power and cooling capacity must be provisioned.

  1. Significance: AI rack power density has grown dramatically. The chapter’s four-node DGX H100 scenario contains 32 GPUs at 700 W each and draws 33.5 kW once host, network, conversion, and allocated cooling overhead are included. This exceeds the roughly 12 kW typical of general-purpose data center racks and requires purpose-designed electrical distribution and cooling capacity. Whether that cooling is engineered air or liquid depends on the system configuration and facility design.
  2. Distinction: A node packages compute; a rack integrates shared power, cooling, and network distribution. Components and nodes remain individually serviceable, but their maintenance and replacement must respect the capacity and failure boundaries of those shared systems.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that rack placement is a logistical afterthought. Nodes within the same rack share a ToR switch and have single-hop connectivity; nodes in different racks cross at least two switch hops. Placing pipeline stages within a rack can reduce inter-rack traffic when the actual paths and available capacity support the stage-boundary exchanges.

2.6.1 Power and thermal envelopes

Sustaining the compute rate that HBM and Tensor Cores make possible requires a power-delivery and cooling system sized for the workload. Delivery itself adds heat before any computation does, because each of the five voltage-conversion stages between the medium-voltage utility grid and the sub-volt GPU die loses part of the power it passes on (figure 12).

The illustrative conversion losses in figure 12 require roughly 1.3 MW upstream to deliver 1 MW to the dies, before heat removal is counted. Facilities summarize that overhead with power usage effectiveness (PUE), total facility power divided by IT-equipment power, and because the IT boundary already contains some of these conversion losses they must not be counted twice. Facility-level power metrics defines the ratio and draws its boundary.

Every floating-point operation dissipates energy as heat. Dense Tensor Core activity during large matrix multiplications drives heat proportional to the switching activity of billions of transistors. Fleet design must pair each accelerator’s sustained power envelope with any documented transient excursion requirement. At dense fleet scale, this produces the power wall, the point at which facility power delivery and heat removal, rather than available arithmetic units, determine how much compute can be installed and sustained.

The thermodynamic limit (principle 4), which holds that every joule a computation draws ends as heat and that a facility can run only as fast as it removes that heat, makes heat removal part of usable compute capacity. A facility can reach its power or cooling envelope while arithmetic capacity is still available to purchase, so the scarce quantity becomes watts per unit of floor area rather than FLOP/s. The power density wall (principle 3) adds that spatial constraint, bounding cluster scaling by the watts each rack can dissipate rather than only by silicon area or floor space. If a proposed rack exceeds the engineered airflow’s heat-removal capability, the design must lower its density or move to liquid, which at the 100 kW class of high-density training racks is part of the rack design rather than an option.

Definition 2.6: Thermal design power (TDP)

Thermal design power (TDP) is a vendor-defined sustained thermal design target in watts for a specified product configuration and operating condition.

  1. Significance: The H100 SXM5 configuration used in this chapter carries a 700 W design target. Sustaining the intended operating point requires a thermal path and facility loop qualified for that target under the vendor’s inlet and coolant conditions.
  2. Distinction: TDP does not denote one universal instantaneous-power maximum. TGP, board power, configurable limits, and transient specifications describe related electrical constraints under conventions that differ by vendor and product.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that the advertised wattage alone specifies rack power and cooling. Fleet design must combine the sustained thermal target, configured power limit, workload behavior, component overhead, and transient-power requirement.

The trajectory of TDP records a scaling regime reaching its limits. For three decades, Dennard scaling6 allowed chip designers to shrink transistors while maintaining approximately constant power density: smaller transistors required lower voltage, so packing more transistors into the same area did not increase heat output per unit area. As voltage scaling and leakage limits ended that regime, architects had to treat energy, cooling, and parallelism as first-order constraints rather than free byproducts of process shrinks (Dennard et al. 1974; Hennessy and Patterson 2019). Since then, each generation of accelerator has had to trade performance against power and heat. The era of “free” performance improvements through process shrinks is over; every gain in throughput now has to be paid for in watts, cooling capacity, or specialization.

6 Dennard scaling: Named after Dennard et al. (1974) at IBM, who showed how MOSFET dimensions, voltage, and current could scale so that power density stayed roughly constant. Later voltage-scaling limits and leakage effects ended that constant-power-density regime; Hennessy and Patterson (2019) describe the resulting post-Dennard power wall as one reason modern architecture turned toward parallelism and specialization. For ML infrastructure, the V100-to-B200 TDP trajectory (300 W to 1,000 W) is an illustration of the post-Dennard planning problem: cooling and power delivery are now co-equal design constraints with the silicon itself.

Dennard, Robert H., Frank H. Gaensslen, Hwa-Nien Yu, Victor L. Rideout, Elias Bassous, and Antoine R. LeBlanc. 1974. “Design of Ion-Implanted MOSFET’s with Very Small Physical Dimensions.” IEEE J. Solid-State Circuits 9 (5): 256–68. https://doi.org/10.1109/jssc.1974.1050511.
Hennessy, John L., and David A. Patterson. 2019. “A New Golden Age for Computer Architecture.” Communications of the ACM 62 (2): 48–60. https://doi.org/10.1145/3282307.

The V100 (2017) operated at 300 W TDP, the A100 (2020) increased to 400 W, the H100 (2022) reached 700 W, and the B200 (2024) draws 1000 W. In seven years, TDP has more than tripled, growing at approximately 19 percent per year.

If this growth rate continued as a planning scenario on a two-year product cadence, a generation after Blackwell would approach 1,400 W per accelerator, and the generation after that would reach about 2,000 W. At that level, a single 8-GPU node would consume 16 kW of GPU power alone, requiring liquid cooling infrastructure (section 2.6.2) that can remove heat at rates approaching those of industrial process cooling equipment. The applicable sustained power envelope is not merely a specification on a data sheet; it propagates through every level of the infrastructure stack. The power-delivery chain, cooling system, rack design, and facility electrical infrastructure must accommodate deployed accelerators, their documented transients, and the plausible envelope of future refresh cycles.

The propagation is visible at both ends of the stack in published data. Vendors publish a power rating for each accelerator package, and figure 13 (a) collects those ratings across 14 NVIDIA, Google, AMD, and Intel parts. The ratings follow each vendor’s own convention (TDP, maximum board power, or maximum TGP), so they bound design envelopes rather than measure consumption, but they climb steadily from one generation to the next. Panel (b) plots the facility power of supercomputers that Epoch AI tracks for AI work, together with the largest one operating at each date (Epoch AI 2026a; Pilz et al. 2025). From 2013 through 2021 that record stayed between 20 MW and 30 MW, a level set by national high-performance computing systems. The Frontier supercomputer raised it to 40 MW in 2022, and AI training clusters then lifted it to about 350 MW by 2025, a 9× increase in three years. Over the same three years the rating of one package rose from the H100’s 700 W TDP to the B300’s 1,400 W maximum TGP, roughly a 2× increase across the two rating conventions. Hotter packages explain only a small part of the jump. The rest comes from the number of accelerators a single system joins together and the facility built to feed them, which is why the power wall at fleet scale is a provisioning problem for the building as much as a thermal problem for the chip.

Pilz, Konstantin F., James Sanders, Robi Rahman, and Lennart Heim. 2025. “Trends in AI Supercomputers.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2504.16026.

Accelerators implement sophisticated power management to operate within their TDP envelope. When the workload does not fully use all SMs (for example, during the communication phase of a training step when the GPUs are waiting for AllReduce to complete), the GPU firmware can disable the clock signal to idle circuitry through clock gating. Clock gating eliminates dynamic switching power and frees thermal headroom for the active SMs. A separate mechanism, dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS), adjusts the chip’s clock frequency and supply voltage to stay within power and thermal limits. During a large matrix multiplication that activates all SMs simultaneously, DVFS reduces the clock frequency to keep total power within TDP. When some SMs are idle, the active SMs can boost frequency because total chip power is below TDP, so the actual clock, and therefore the actual throughput, varies throughout a training step.

Dynamic power management means that the actual power draw of a GPU varies continuously during training. A typical training step might see power swing from 400 W (during communication phases when most SMs are idle) to 700 W (during the forward and backward pass when all Tensor Cores are active) and back, with each transition occurring in microseconds. The voltage regulator modules (VRMs) on the baseboard, which convert the board supply to the sub-volt core voltage, must respond to these transitions fast enough to maintain a stable supply voltage despite the rapidly changing current draw.

DVFS also couples software optimization to the power envelope. A kernel that achieves high SM occupancy (many active warps per SM) draws more power, potentially triggering a frequency reduction that partially offsets the occupancy benefit. Conversely, a kernel with moderate occupancy may run at a higher clock frequency, achieving comparable throughput at lower power. Power-normalized throughput (TFLOP/s per watt) is therefore a more robust comparison metric than raw TFLOP/s, and it explains why the same GPU can show different sustained clock frequencies depending on the workload and the cooling solution.

At fleet scale, DVFS also affects power planning. Average workload draw can fall below the configured limit because communication phases and incomplete occupancy leave circuitry idle, but averages do not replace sustained and transient design cases. Some operators deliberately lower accelerator power caps to fit more devices within a fixed facility budget, accepting workload-dependent throughput loss in exchange for lower per-device draw. Power capping is a form of the compute-efficiency trade-off at the operational level.

To appreciate the physical challenge, consider what 700 W means in terms of heat flux. An H100 die measures approximately 814 mm² (roughly \(29{\times}28\) mm). Dissipating 700 W from this area produces a heat flux of approximately 86 W/cm². For comparison, the surface of an electric stovetop burner at high heat produces approximately 10 W/cm². An H100 die generates 8.6× the heat flux of a stove burner, concentrated on an area the size of a large postage stamp.

The B200’s 1,000 W envelope spans a dual-die package. In this comparison, the average heat flux remains comparable while the package heat-removal rate increases by 43 percent. Cooling must keep junction temperature and memory temperature within the applicable product limits under the specified inlet and load conditions. A single temperature threshold cannot stand in for those separate requirements.

The thermal interface material (TIM) between the package and its cooling assembly is one part of that heat-transfer path, and it is the part that ages. Training alternates compute-dense phases with communication phases, so the die heats and cools many times per minute; under that cycling, a phase-change or paste TIM can pump out (migrate away from the contact surface) or crack. Increased thermal resistance can raise temperature at the same load, but the performance effect depends on the remaining thermal margin and the device’s control policy. A temperature trend alone does not identify the cause; inlet conditions, flow, contact, and workload also require inspection. When thermal limits reduce clocks without stopping execution, the result is a gray failure, a performance degradation that monitoring must distinguish from an application or network bottleneck.

Arithmetic per watt offsets only part of rising TDP, and a larger capability target can increase total work despite improved efficiency, so facility planning must compare the proposed device count, power envelope, and completion time together. Packing accelerators close together shortens communication paths but concentrates heat. A four-node DGX H100 rack already draws 33.5 kW including its allocated cooling overhead, several times the load of a conventional server rack. The accelerators of a 10,000-GPU cluster, the round fleet size this chapter uses for warehouse-scale examples, alone draw 7 MW before hosts, network, and cooling are counted. Rack and pod designs must reconcile that proximity with enough space and infrastructure to deliver power and remove heat across all participating nodes.

Power-envelope implications recur throughout the infrastructure hierarchy. At the node level, configured limits constrain how many accelerators can share a chassis (section 2.5). At the rack level, aggregate sustained load and heat flux help determine whether engineered air or liquid cooling can meet the design point (section 2.6.2). At the pod level, coincident demand and transient behavior shape megawatt-scale power delivery (section 2.7). Every level above the die must therefore manage both sustained heat and electrical variation. Accelerator selection must compare useful workload throughput within that facility envelope, rather than treating power as a cost added after the compute decision.

Power delivery networks and transient dynamics

Fitting redundant AC power supply units inside every eight-GPU chassis consumes enclosure volume needed for cooling manifolds and high-speed interconnects, especially as rack loads climb toward 100 kW in liquid-cooled clusters. Many dense-rack designs therefore replace per-chassis AC supplies with rack-level DC power distribution. In this architecture, a centralized power shelf converts mains AC to a 48V DC busbar that runs the height of the rack. Power reaches the server backplane through the 48V bus, and local DC-to-DC converters supply the lower voltages required by the components. Consolidation can recover chassis volume for cooling loops and high-bandwidth interconnects, but it does not by itself establish fewer conversion stages or a fixed efficiency gain. The comparison must account for each design’s actual stages and conversion efficiency across the intended load range. Shared power shelves and busbars also create a rack-level failure boundary whose redundancy and serviceability must be qualified.

A training step makes each accelerator’s current draw jump. When a collective barrier releases a node’s GPUs from a communication phase into dense arithmetic, each one steps from a few hundred watts toward its full power envelope within microseconds, and the barrier correlates those steps across the node. The power delivery network (PDN) must hold the supply voltage steady through that step, and the difficulty grows as the voltage falls from the 48V busbar to the sub-1V rail at the die.

At a fixed power, current scales inversely with voltage, so the core rail carries far more current than the busbar; the voltage regulators transform voltage and current between those boundaries. What disturbs the supply is the rate of change of that current, its slew rate \(d\AcceleratorSupplyCurrent/d\TimeInstant\). Every conductor loop has inductance, a property that resists changes in current, so a fast current step produces a momentary voltage dip, or droop, even when average power remains within the facility budget.

Definition 2.7: Power delivery network (PDN) and transient dynamics

Power delivery network (PDN) is the electrical distribution hierarchy that supplies the processor through regulators, interconnects, and decoupling capacitors. Parasitic loop inductance contributes to voltage fluctuations during changes in current.

  1. Significance: Synchronized workload transitions can correlate accelerator demand. Supply excursions outside the device’s specified limits can cause timing errors, protective throttling, or resets.
  2. Distinction: Local electrical transients require a faster response than facility power-control loops can provide. Regulators and nearby charge storage address different timescales within the same hierarchy.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that the full core-current slew acts on a single inductance summed from rack to die. Regulation and decoupling change the current waveform at each stage; the relevant inductance and current must belong to the same path.

For a particular inductive path, the voltage excursion contributed by its inductance follows equation 2:

\[\FiniteChange \SupplyVoltage= \ParasiticLoopInductance\frac{d\AcceleratorSupplyCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} \tag{2}\]

Here \(\ParasiticLoopInductance\) is the loop inductance of that path, and \(d\AcceleratorSupplyCurrent/d\TimeInstant\) is the current slew through it. Local capacitors initially supply part of a load-current increase, so the current slew seen upstream need not equal the slew at the core. A complete rail model also accounts for resistance, capacitance, and regulator response.

A worked estimate for one accelerator shows why the hierarchy matters. When an H100 moves from its communication-phase draw of about 400 W to its 700 W envelope on a 0.8 V core rail, its core current rises by about 375 A. Suppose the board loop between the voltage regulator and the package has 50 pH of inductance. If package capacitors supply the fast edge so that this loop sees the step over 1 μs, equation 2 gives a droop of about 19 mV, or 2.3 percent of the rail. If the same step reached that loop in 100 ns, the droop would be about 190 mV, roughly 23 percent of the rail and far outside a 5 percent operating band. The inputs are scenario assumptions, but the tenfold sensitivity to rise time is physical. The same current step stays inside tolerance or corrupts timing depending on which charge store answers it first.

The collective barrier that aligns transitions from communication to arithmetic raises the correlation of demand across a rack, but it does not imply that every accelerator switches on the same nanosecond or that their core currents flow through one shared low-voltage loop. Engineers must evaluate both each device’s local voltage excursion and the aggregate demand seen by upstream supplies. A rising load can produce voltage droop, where droop below the specified operating minimum can invalidate timing margins, while an abrupt load reduction can produce overshoot that must remain within the device’s specified limits as well. Neither the magnitude nor the resulting failure mode follows from workload synchronization alone. The mitigation is a hierarchy of charge storage and control, in which each tier answers the transients too fast for the tier above it (table 13), with placement and parasitic impedance determining which frequencies each tier can address.

The facility row of table 13 is where the barrier’s correlation appears in aggregate. For an illustrative calculation, assume 1,024 GPUs change together from 400 W to 700 W each over 100 μs. The assumed aggregate step is 307 kW, giving a ramp rate of 3.07 GW/s. This is a load-side scenario, not a measured utility input. No single regulator carries this step, because each accelerator draws its share through its own board, yet the shared upstream supplies see the sum.

↪ How energy buffering presents this ramp to the grid as a smoother, more easily connected load is developed in Power delivery.

Workload-level controls complement these hardware tiers. Power ramping can reduce abrupt demand changes where the hardware and firmware support it, but a design must verify those controls rather than assume that every kernel launch receives automatic smoothing. The fleet-level lesson is that average power capacity and transient voltage integrity require separate checks.

2.6.2 Cooling class and heat removal

The power path ends in heat. Under the thermodynamic limit (principle 4), nearly every joule an accelerator draws leaves as heat through switching, leakage, and resistive losses. During a transient some of that energy warms the chip and its surroundings, but in steady operation the stored thermal energy stops rising, so cooling must remove heat at about the rate the rack consumes electricity. When removal falls behind generation, temperature climbs through the available thermal margin until throttling lowers clocks and throughput, and sustained overheating can damage the hardware. The rack’s cooling class, air or liquid, is the choice of fluid that carries this heat away, and it bounds how dense the rack can be.

When cooling falls behind, temperature climbs through the thermal margin until throttling caps it.

Heat transfer explains why density reshapes the rack. Air carries far less heat per unit volume than liquid, so an air-cooled rack needs enough airflow, pressure, and temperature rise to carry away its heat. How much a hall can supply depends on the server and facility design rather than on one rack-power threshold.

Air cooling, the dominant technology for decades, works by blowing room-temperature air across heat sinks attached to the chips. The air absorbs heat at a rate determined by its specific heat capacity, roughly 1.0 kJ/kg/K. The heated air is exhausted from the rear of the rack, typically 15–20 degrees Celsius warmer than the inlet, and directed to a computer room air conditioning (CRAC) unit that cools it before recirculating.

Air is a poor thermal conductor. Its thermal conductivity is only 0.026 W/m/K, compared to 0.6 W/m/K for water and 400 W/m/K for copper. Removing a large heat load with air requires substantial volumetric flow. Fan power depends on that flow, pressure drop, and fan efficiency; rack power alone does not specify a fixed cooling fraction.

The heat that a coolant stream removes depends on how much fluid flows, how much heat each kilogram absorbs per degree of warming, and how far the fluid warms (equation 3):

\[\CoolantHeatLoad = \CoolantMassFlowRate\times \SpecificHeatCapacity\times \FiniteChange\CoolantTemperature \tag{3}\]

where \(\CoolantHeatLoad\) is the heat removed (watts), \(\CoolantMassFlowRate\) is the mass flow rate (kg/s), \(\SpecificHeatCapacity\) is the specific heat capacity (J/kg/K), and \(\FiniteChange\CoolantTemperature\) is the temperature difference between outlet and inlet. For air with \(\SpecificHeatCapacity\) of 1,005 J/kg/K and a typical \(\FiniteChange\CoolantTemperature\) of 15 K (inlet at 20 degrees C, outlet at 35 degrees C), removing 100 kW requires a mass flow rate of 100,000 \(\div\) (1,005 \(\times\) 15) \(\approx\) 6.6 kg/s. At sea-level air density of 1.2 kg/m³, this corresponds to a volumetric flow rate of 5.5 m³/s, or approximately 11,713 CFM (cubic feet per minute). Moving this much air through the confined space of a server rack requires powerful fans that themselves consume substantial power.

At higher power densities, the airflow and pressure required to cool a rack raise fan energy and can make air cooling impractical before the IT load itself reaches a power limit. The exact crossover depends on rack geometry, inlet temperature, pressure drop, and fan-control policy rather than on one universal rack-power threshold.
