Water has a specific heat capacity of 4.18 kJ/kg/K, over four times that of air, and a thermal conductivity roughly 23× higher. Its much greater density also lets a small fluid volume carry substantial heat. To appreciate the magnitude of the difference, consider how much fluid must flow to remove 700 W from a single GPU. Air at a 15-degree temperature rise requires tens of liters per second of airflow (a small wind tunnel). Water at the same temperature rise requires only about 0.01 liters per second (a thin stream). This thousands-fold difference in volumetric flow permits compact liquid paths, but it does not determine pumping energy. Pressure drop through cold plates, pipes, and fittings, together with pump efficiency, determines the power needed to sustain that flow.

At rack scale the same equation sizes the liquid loop. Removing the modeled 120 kW load of a GB200 NVL72 rack through water that warms by 10 °C takes about 170 liters per minute, and a smaller temperature rise needs proportionally more flow. A real design must use the heat fraction the liquid loop actually captures and account for residual air cooling; flow capacity, pressure, leak containment, and loss-of-flow response are separate requirements, which the facility controls of section 2.6.4 treat as failure domains.

Direct-to-chip liquid cooling routes coolant through cold plates mounted on GPU packages. The cold plate contacts the heat spreader through thermal interface material; the resulting thermal resistance depends on the package, interface, plate design, and flow. The coolant carries heat through manifolds and pipes to a coolant distribution unit (CDU) at the rack or row level. The permissible supply temperature depends on the equipment, so liquid cooling need not require chilled water.

In a two-loop design, the CDU transfers heat from the chip-level coolant to the facility water loop, which carries it toward cooling towers or dry coolers. Isolation lets each loop maintain its required fluid chemistry and pressure. The chip-level coolant must satisfy the equipment’s compatibility and cleanliness requirements to control deposits, corrosion, and blockage; purity alone does not establish suitability. Liquid cooling can reduce server airflow requirements, although memory modules (DIMMs), voltage regulator modules (VRMs), and other components without cold plates may still need fans, and lower fan demand can also reduce noise.

A more aggressive approach, immersion cooling, submerges entire server boards in a tank of nonconductive dielectric fluid. The fluid absorbs heat from wetted surfaces, replacing server airflow; high-heat-flux components may still need heat sinks or directed flow, and flow, local heat flux, material compatibility, and heat-exchanger capacity still determine component temperatures. Single-phase immersion cooling uses a fluid that remains liquid throughout the process, with heat carried away by convection currents in the tank. The heated fluid rises to the surface, is pumped through a heat exchanger to reject the heat to the building’s chilled water loop, and returns to the bottom of the tank. Two-phase immersion cooling takes this further: fluid boils at heated surfaces, carries latent heat to a condenser, and returns as liquid, with heat removal depending on the fluid, pressure, condenser capacity, and operating conditions. The complete facility still spends energy on heat rejection and electrical distribution, so the cooling mechanism alone does not settle facility energy.

The trade-off is serviceability. Accessing a failed component requires handling fluid-wetted hardware, and the dielectric fluids themselves are expensive ($20–$50 per liter). A single immersion tank holding four server boards may contain 500–1,000 liters of fluid, representing $10,000–$50,000 in coolant cost alone. The operational procedures for immersion-cooled facilities differ sharply from air-cooled ones, requiring specialized training for technicians and different approaches to cable management, since all wetted materials must be compatible with prolonged fluid exposure. Depending on the fluid and its additives, metals can corrode, while polymer insulation and seals can swell or degrade. Qualifying compatible materials adds engineering work and can constrain component choices. These serviceability costs enter the design decision alongside the heat paths, binding limits, and parasitic loads of the air and liquid regimes (table 14).

Direct-to-chip liquid cooling supports higher heat flux than forced air because cold plates route chip heat directly into circulating fluid with high volumetric heat capacity before reaching facility heat rejection, whereas air systems rely on convective dissipation across finned heat sinks (figure 14). Ambient wet-bulb temperature (the lowest temperature that evaporation can bring outdoor air to, which bounds evaporative heat rejection) and rack power density determine whether an air, liquid, or hybrid loop satisfies the chip junction temperature envelope.

Two anchors bound the choice for this chapter’s racks. The four-node DGX H100 scenario at 33.5 kW can use engineered air cooling in a suitable facility, while racks in the 100 kW class of high-density training racks need liquid, which raises heat-removal capacity and can reduce fan energy. Between the two anchors, the facility’s airflow capacity decides. Cooling class is therefore a configuration constraint. Rack layout and heat-removal capacity, not accelerator count alone, determine whether the intended configuration can sustain its workload or must reduce density or power.

Engineered air can still cool the DGX rack; the 100 kW class needs liquid.

↪ What heat removal adds to facility energy (PUE), and the water that heat rejection consumes, are developed in Cooling energy, heat rejection, and water.

2.6.3 Rack design considerations

Packing 33.5 kW of compute and thermal hardware into a standard 42U frame leaves almost no room for conventional server layouts. In an enterprise 42U rack, servers are installed as independent 1U or 2U pizza-box units, each with its own fans, power supplies, and cable connections. A single DGX H100 instead occupies 8U and contains 8 GPUs, the NVSwitch fabric, multiple power supplies, and (in liquid-cooled configurations) coolant manifolds with quick-connect fittings. Four DGX units fill 32U of a 42U rack, leaving only 10U for networking switches, cable management, and PDUs.

The transition from traditional server form factors to ML-optimized designs is driven by the same power density challenge that motivates engineered airflow or liquid cooling. A traditional 1U server dissipates 300–500 W and requires only modest airflow for cooling. A DGX H100 node dissipates approximately 8.4 kW across its 8 GPUs, NVSwitches, and supporting components, requiring either high-volume forced airflow (in air-cooled configurations) or liquid cooling plumbing (in liquid-cooled configurations). The form factor must accommodate both the compute hardware and the thermal management infrastructure, which is why ML nodes are substantially taller (8U vs. 1U) than traditional servers.

Cable management is a nontrivial engineering challenge. Each DGX H100 node has eight InfiniBand cables (one per GPU), two Ethernet management cables, power cables, and, for liquid-cooled units, coolant hoses. A fully populated rack contains over 40 high-speed cables whose layout is governed by three strict physical constraints: airflow clearance, minimum bend radius, and electromagnetic interference. In air-cooled designs, cables must not obstruct intake or exhaust airflow, because even a localized blockage creates hot spots that trigger thermal throttling. High-speed cables simultaneously enforce a minimum bend radius (for installed fiber optic cable, typically 10–15\(\times\) the outer diameter, per the manufacturer’s specification (The Fiber Optic Association 2020)), below which optical fibers suffer attenuation and induce bit errors or complete link drops. Densely bundling high-speed copper cables can also induce electromagnetic crosstalk that degrades signal quality. In large installations, the cable plant alone can take weeks to install and test, and cable routing errors are one of the most common causes of post-installation debugging delays.

The Fiber Optic Association. 2020. Fiber Optic Cable Bend Radius or Diameter.

The rack’s network attachment needs the same density-driven rethinking, starting with the top-of-rack (ToR) switch. In traditional data centers, a ToR switch provides 1–10 Gb/s Ethernet connectivity to each server, aggregated into uplinks to higher-level switches. In an ML rack, the ToR switch is an InfiniBand or high-speed Ethernet switch providing 400 Gb/s per port, and it must handle the bursty, synchronized traffic patterns of distributed training. In a conventional design all eight GPUs of a node share one ToR switch, so a data-parallel AllReduce converges on that switch and its uplinks. A rail-optimized design instead gives each GPU index its own leaf switch (GPU 0 of every node on leaf 0, GPU 1 on leaf 1, and so on), so same-index collectives stay on one switch. The price is extra switches and cabling, and the benefit depends on how the job’s process groups map onto the rails.

↪ How rail-optimized topologies align cabling with collective traffic is developed in Rail-optimized topology.

2.6.4 Facility reliability and environmental controls

A coolant leak or electrical fault in a single DGX H100 rack threatens $1.4 million in hardware and can halt a multi-rack training job across the upper levels of the failure-domain hierarchy introduced in section 2.5.5. Data centers housing ML infrastructure therefore pair physical security with continuous environmental monitoring that becomes part of the compute design rather than a facilities afterthought. Beneath raised floor tiles and along liquid cooling piping, water sensors identify leaks within seconds and close automatic coolant isolation valves, while humidity controls keep the air within a moderate band, dry enough to prevent condensation and humid enough to limit static discharge. Inside the cabinets, aspirating smoke detectors sample rack air continuously to catch combustion byproducts at concentrations far below what sprinkler-activating detectors can sense. Vibration sensors on the building structure and rack frames flag seismic events or construction shocks before they can damage disk drives or loosen cable connections.

Cooling failures threaten both hardware and useful fleet work. When coolant flow stops, a dense rack loses heat-removal capacity and must throttle or shut down before thermal runaway. N+1 redundancy in CDUs, temperature monitoring, and automatic load shedding limit the affected capacity. An emergency air path can support reduced-power operation where the facility design provides one.

Serviceability also changes the failure budget. Leaks can damage equipment and drain cooling capacity, which is why the leak sensors above drive isolation valves, while quick-disconnect fittings allow node replacement without draining the entire loop. Their seals and coolant compatibility therefore belong in the maintenance plan. The fleet-level consequence depends on how much synchronized work shares the failed cooling domain.

The economics of cooling reliability shift when moving from independent inference servers to tightly coupled training clusters. In a distributed training run using synchronous parallelism, a cooling-loop failure that idles one slice of the cluster can halt the entire job. Consider a cooling failure that triggers a thermal shutdown of the 256 GPUs served by one coolant distribution unit within a 10,000-GPU cluster, about ten times the 1,024-GPU reference cluster. The example counts lost compute capacity, excluding repair and replacement costs. If the repair time for a CDU pump is 4 hours, the immediate loss of 256 GPUs at the fleet’s reference in-service rate of $2 per GPU-hour is $2,048. Assuming the job cannot continue elastically and the remaining 9,744 GPUs remain unavailable for other work, the synchronous blast radius inflates the cost to $80,000 in lost compute time. Adding checkpoint retrieval and rollback of 30 to 60 minutes of lost computation brings the illustrative lost-compute total to $90,000–$100,000 before any additional recovery delay. Whether N+1 cooling redundancy is worth its price follows from comparing these expected outage losses and recovery behavior with that price, rather than from treating redundancy as an unconditional requirement. The energy the rollback repeats belongs to the facility energy account of Cooling energy, heat rejection, and water.

One cooling failure can idle the whole training job.

Coolant condition is another reliability input. Deionized water and compatible inhibitors control galvanic corrosion, the damage caused by electrochemical differences between metals in the loop. Biofilm on heat-transfer surfaces impedes cooling and increases pumping demand. Maintenance must preserve coolant chemistry and heat-transfer performance, because a fouled or corroding loop loses capacity before it fails outright.

These controls bear directly on reliability and uptime. A fire in a single rack can shut down an entire data center hall for days while the fire suppression system is recharged and the affected area is decontaminated. The 2021 OVHcloud fire in Strasbourg shows how a single-building incident, absent the right compartmentalization, escalates into a site-wide failure domain.

War Story 2.1: The data center that became the failure domain (2021)
Context: OVHcloud operated four data centers at its Strasbourg site, serving customers whose systems depended on the site’s physical power, fire, and recovery design (OVHcloud 2021).

Mechanism: On March 10, 2021, a fire broke out in building SBG2. Uncontained fire spread and shared power pathways destroyed building SBG2, damaged adjacent SBG1, and forced emergency shutdown of SBG3 and SBG4.

Impact: Thousands of servers were physically destroyed, taking over 3.6 million websites offline worldwide and destroying unbacked-up customer data.

Fix: OVHcloud rebuilt the site with independent physical fire barriers, isolated power distribution per building, and mandatory free off-site backup storage.

Systems lesson: Compute infrastructure extends beyond GPUs and network topology. Fire compartments, power isolation, backup geography, and disaster-recovery assumptions determine whether a rack-scale incident becomes a site-scale outage. Multi-node ML training clusters inherit this physical dependency: a single data-center power or cooling domain failure halts active training runs and destroys un-replicated checkpoint states unless recovery paths cross physical facility boundaries.

OVHcloud. 2021. Strasbourg Datacentre: Latest Information. OVHcloud incident update.
Uptime Institute. 2026. Tier Classification System. Uptime Institute web page.

Once rack-level controls are in place, the reliability question moves up to facility redundancy, where the same failure-domain logic that isolates a leaking rack also decides how much duplicate power, cooling, and recovery capacity the site should buy. Data center reliability is commonly classified using the Uptime Institute Tier system, with Tier III and Tier IV representing common options for mission-critical compute (Uptime Institute 2026). A Tier III facility is concurrently maintainable: redundant capacity components and distribution paths let operators maintain or replace equipment without shutting down IT operations. Tier IV adds fault tolerance through independent, physically isolated systems, so a single equipment failure or distribution-path interruption should not affect IT operations. The practical difference is not a magic availability number; it is how much failure prevention the building buys in electrical and mechanical infrastructure before software-level resilience takes over.

For ML training, the calculus differs from traditional enterprise computing. A training run is a long-running batch process, not a real-time transaction system. A facility outage during a two-week run therefore usually costs a restart from the last checkpoint rather than permanent data loss, provided a valid checkpoint survives in an accessible failure domain and usable compute becomes available. The restart still incurs lost work, recovery time, and potentially a missed deadline. Many training deployments therefore pair concurrently maintainable (Tier III) facilities with aggressive application-level fault tolerance. Rather than buying every layer of building-level redundancy, they invest in checkpointing, job restart, and storage that can absorb rare facility interruptions, and redirect the avoided resiliency spend toward accelerators, networking, or storage. Facility redundancy and application-level recovery address different parts of the same risk. A design can trade some preventive infrastructure for checkpointing and recovery capacity only after checking which failures the recovery path survives and whether the resulting completion-time risk is acceptable.

Checkpoint 2.3: Infrastructure physics

A team is planning to deploy 256 H100 GPUs (32 nodes, each drawing about 8.4 kW as computed in section 2.6.3) in an existing air-cooled data center with 250 kW of available facility power, a PUE of 1.5, and a cooling plant that can reject 200 kW of IT heat.

The rack concentrates power and heat into a physical volume where thermodynamics, not software, sets the limits. A single rack of 32 GPUs, however, is far from sufficient for our 175B model, which needs tens to hundreds of nodes, many racks’ worth, to train on a practical schedule. The next level of infrastructure aggregates tens to hundreds of racks into a unified computing system: the pod, whose physical interconnect fabric must be engineered around the communication patterns and failure domains the workload creates.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A four-node DGX H100 rack draws approximately \(33\text{ kW}\) of IT power. Which heat-transfer property can make direct-to-chip liquid cooling attractive as rack density rises, even though engineered air cooling remains possible in a suitable facility?

    1. Liquid cooling increases GPU arithmetic intensity by lowering electrical resistance inside Tensor Cores.
    2. Water has much higher volumetric heat capacity than air, so it can carry away the same heat with a much smaller volumetric flow for a comparable temperature rise.
    3. Air cooling causes optical transceivers to melt when exposed to fan airflow velocities above 5 meters per second.
    4. High-density racks require full dielectric immersion because cold plates cannot conduct heat from silicon heat spreaders.

  2. A facilities team provisions its cooling and electrical infrastructure for the average GPU power observed during training (\(\approx 500\text{ W}\) per GPU) rather than the \(700\text{ W}\) sustained design target. Explain why this choice can silently degrade cluster training throughput even when no hardware errors or crashes occur.

  3. Synchronous ML training can correlate accelerator power ramps across a cluster. How does this electrical behavior distinguish its power-delivery requirements from those of largely uncorrelated web-serving workloads?

    1. ML clusters operate on direct current exclusively, eliminating the need for uninterruptible power supplies.
    2. Web-serving data centers experience higher \(di/dt\) transients because individual HTTP requests draw more peak current than matrix multiplications.
    3. ML training consumes purely reactive power that causes step-down transformers to freeze mechanically.
    4. Correlated demand changes require checking both local regulator and decoupling response and the aggregate demand seen by upstream supplies, rather than sizing solely for average power.

  4. True or False: Replacing individual server AC supplies with a centralized 48V DC power shelf and busbar guarantees one fewer conversion stage and a 2–3% electrical efficiency gain.

  5. A facility aisle is qualified to remove a sustained 25 kW of IT heat per rack with its existing air-cooling design under the specified inlet temperature and airflow. The proposed AI workload measures 60 kW per rack over its sustained operating interval. Which observation establishes the need to change the cooling design or reduce the rack load?

    1. Web workloads have higher peak memory bandwidth requirements, causing localized thermal spikes.
    2. AI training generates bursty, millisecond-scale CPU spikes followed by long idle periods, creating thermal cycling fatigue.
    3. The measured 60 kW rack load exceeds this aisle’s qualified 25 kW air-cooling envelope; a qualified liquid, hybrid, or redesigned air system, or a lower rack load, must restore thermal feasibility.
    4. AI models require lower ambient operating temperatures to maintain FP16 arithmetic precision.

  6. Order the thermal stages of heat removal in a direct-to-chip liquid-cooled data center, from the silicon die to the external environment: (1) heat transfers through a thin thermal interface material (TIM) into a copper cold plate, (2) the Coolant Distribution Unit (CDU) heat exchanger transfers thermal energy from the closed rack loop to the facility secondary loop, (3) external cooling towers or dry coolers reject heat into the ambient outdoor atmosphere, (4) liquid coolant absorbs heat as it flows through microchannels directly above the accelerator package, (5) active switching in transistor junctions generates heat on the silicon die.

See Answers →

2.7 The Pod

Training our 175B model on a single DGX H100 node (8 GPUs, 15,832 TFLOP/s of aggregate FP8 peak) would take about 1.4 years at the efficiency assumed below, even if the model state could fit. Roughly 37 nodes bring the compute-only time down to two weeks, and about 512 nodes bring it to a single day, before any communication or recovery overhead. A pod is the cluster-scale unit that makes this possible: many racks under one high-speed fabric, power envelope, and cooling design. The engineering challenge is wiring those nodes together into a network fast enough to keep gradient synchronization from becoming the bottleneck, so that the fabric serves the role a system bus serves within a single machine. Pod design is the first point where topology, power, and failure domains become shared infrastructure rather than per-rack concerns. Two fabric families dominate: a switched fat tree supplies full bisection bandwidth across node uplinks, while a direct-connect torus wires each node to its neighbors, using fewer switches but giving bisection bandwidth that grows more slowly than node count. Fat-tree (Clos) networks and Dragonfly and torus alternatives develop both.

The pod-scale examples that follow deliberately step up to eight times the 1,024-GPU reference cluster. A 1,024-node DGX H100 cluster contains 8,192 GPUs, 4,096 NVSwitch chips, 8,192 InfiniBand HCAs, several hundred InfiniBand switches, tens of thousands of cables, and draws approximately 8.6 MW of rack power, including each rack’s allocated cooling overhead. It occupies 256 four-node racks across one or more data center halls.

The physical weight of such a cluster is also substantial. Each DGX H100 node weighs approximately 130 kg (288 lb), so 1,024 nodes weigh over 133 metric tons. Racks, switches, cables, and cooling infrastructure add substantially to that total. The data center floor must be structurally engineered to support this concentrated load, which is far denser than the loading that traditional server rooms were designed for. This structural requirement is often overlooked during facility planning and can be a blocking constraint for retrofitting existing buildings.

The physical layout of the data center hall reflects these density constraints. The high power density of ML training racks requires strict hot aisle/cold aisle containment in air-cooled sections, where cold air is forced into the enclosed front of the rack and waste heat is captured immediately at the rear exhaust. The “spine” of the hall, the central cable corridor connecting all racks to the aggregation switches, must accommodate thousands of fiber optic cables and power feeds. Overhead cable trays are preferred over under-floor routing to improve airflow and accessibility, carrying the heavy copper power feeds and fragile fiber interconnects that form the nervous system of the cluster. The layout must also accommodate the liquid cooling infrastructure: coolant distribution unit placement, coolant piping runs, and isolation valves that allow individual racks to be serviced without draining the entire cooling loop. These physical layout decisions, made during facility design, constrain the network topology options available to the training team years later.

Training a 175B model on this cluster costs about \(6 \times \Parameters\times \DatasetSize\) floating-point operations for \(\Parameters\) parameters and \(\DatasetSize\) training tokens, the training-compute estimate of Compute-optimal resource allocation. The token count is where the scaling laws of AI Scaling Laws enter the hardware plan, because a compute-optimal allocation would give a model of this size many times more tokens than GPT-3 saw, multiplying this budget, and the pod-hours it consumes, by the same factor. Notebook 2.4 turns the budget into a compute-only baseline under an assumed local compute efficiency. That efficiency is not measured distributed MFU, because MFU over a distributed step already includes its communication and pipeline losses. On the 1,024-GPU reference cluster itself the same baseline would take about 4 days. In the terms of the Fleet Law (equation 1.3), that baseline is the \(\IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount\) term alone.

In practice, communication overhead, pipeline bubbles, checkpoint I/O, hardware failures, and maintenance windows compound to extend the actual wall-clock time beyond that baseline. The first two are the Fleet Law’s exposed \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) and \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\) terms, and the rest are operational losses around the step. The notebook chains these factors into a scenario estimate of wall-clock time; production schedules can stretch further once queueing, data readiness, debugging, reruns, and site-specific operational margins enter the plan. At pod scale, the schedule therefore depends on fleet overhead as well as on local execution, and recovering the lost time draws on better networks, overlapped communication, and proactive maintenance.

Napkin Math 2.4: Training time for 175B
The training time for our 175B model derives from first principles.

  1. Total FLOPs: Using the same \(6 \times \Parameters\times \DatasetSize\) approximation, where \(\Parameters = 175 \times 10^9\) and \(\DatasetSize = 300 \times 10^9\) tokens: \(3.15 \times 10^{23}\) FLOPs

  2. Cluster throughput: 8,192 H100 GPUs at 1,979 TFLOP/s peak, assuming 45 percent local compute efficiency before distributed overhead: \(8,192 \times 1979 \times 10^{12} \times 0.45\) FLOP/s before distributed overhead \(\approx\) 7.30 EFLOP/s

  3. Compute-only training baseline: (\(3.15 \times 10^{23}\) FLOPs)/(\(8,192 \times 1979 \times 10^{12} \times 0.45\) FLOP/s) \(\div 3600 \approx\) 12.0 hours

  4. Illustrative overhead chain: The scaling factor already includes pipeline fill and drain, so no separate bubble step follows. Each subsequent percentage adds time relative to the preceding subtotal, not to final wall time; the factors are scenario assumptions, not independent measured losses:

    • Distributed-step losses (scaling efficiency 0.56, the modeled tier for 8,192 GPUs, covering communication and pipeline bubbles): \(\div\) 0.56 \(\rightarrow\) 21.4 hours
    • Checkpoint overhead (\(\approx 3\%\)): \(\times 1.03 \rightarrow\) 22.1 hours
    • Hardware failures and restarts (\(\approx 10\%\) of the checkpoint-adjusted subtotal): \(\times 1.10 \rightarrow\) 24.3 hours
    • Maintenance windows (\(\approx 5\%\)): \(\times 1.05 \rightarrow\) 25.5 hours

The total training time is 25.5 hours in this simplified scenario chain, or about 1.1 days. Real production calendars may still stretch to weeks after queueing, debugging, reruns, and site-specific operational margins are included. Recovering this lost time is the domain of distributed systems engineering.

2.7.1 The pod as a warehouse-scale computer

When a single training job spans 10,000 H100 GPUs across an entire data center hall, about ten times the reference cluster, a slow network tier or a single device failure stalls the whole building’s computation. At this scale, the pod operates as a warehouse-scale computer (WSC) (Barroso et al. 2019) and requires a matched budget for arithmetic, communication, and recovery. Rounding the pod up to 10,000 GPUs keeps the warehouse-scale arithmetic easy to follow, and the chapter’s other fleet-scale power, cost, and failure examples use the same round figure. Such a WSC supplies approximately 9.89 EFLOP/s of FP16 peak and 19.8 EFLOP/s at FP8. Non-blocking InfiniBand at 50 GB/s per GPU, the injection bandwidth each endpoint can push into the fabric, provides 500 TB/s of aggregate injection bandwidth and 250 TB/s across the bisection, the cut that splits the cluster into two equal halves. With 4:1 oversubscription at that boundary, meaning the switch tier above it has one quarter as much uplink as downlink capacity, the modeled crossing capacity falls to 62.5 TB/s. The effect on a collective depends on how much traffic crosses the constrained paths, its schedule, and competing transfers. The bandwidth ratio alone is not a collective-time multiplier.

↩︎ The warehouse-scale computer as the unit of fleet design is established in The shift to the warehouse-scale computer.

Treating the building as one computer means that a server assigned to a distributed training job contributes to a shared completion time, and the network fabric, distributed storage, and fleet orchestrator make the building’s resources available as one training system. Rack placement, the power delivery chain, and the network topology must therefore be co-designed, because a mistake at any layer propagates through the others. The first physical consequence concerns communication: the network can set the scaling ceiling. Within a single node NVLink supplies enough bandwidth for tensor parallelism, but across nodes the fabric must carry gradient tensors, activation checkpoints, and pipeline stage outputs, so a building-scale machine is only as fast as the wires that bind its cores. The WSC reframing makes the interconnect a first-class architectural component rather than a peripheral.

The second consequence is that failure is a physical certainty, not an exceptional event. Under the scenario assumption of independent failures and a per-GPU MTTF of approximately 50,000 hours, a cluster of 10,000 GPUs has a mean interval of roughly five hours between GPU failures, and the 25K-GPU fleet of figure 1 about two. That is an expectation, not a periodic schedule, and shared power or cooling faults add correlated exposure. The node failure estimate of section 2.5.5 and the facility controls of section 2.6.4 are the same exposure at smaller scope. Redundant infrastructure, such as dual-path power feeds and redundant switch fabrics, can prevent some interruptions; checkpoint storage and recovery capacity limit lost progress when a running job must stop. Both belong in the physical design. Redundancy keeps many component failures from halting the job, and checkpoints bound the work lost when one does.

The third consequence is that the building blocks combine differently depending on the workload they must carry. A pod is not assembled from a single canonical part list; the interconnect, the switching, and the memory placement are each chosen against the communication and capacity pattern the dominant workload imposes. Three pod designs show the range (table 15). An NVIDIA SuperPOD wires GPUs through an InfiniBand fat-tree for any-to-any flexibility, a Google TPU pod hard-wires chips into a torus for dataflow throughput, and a recommendation pod built for Archetype B arranges the same class of accelerators around embedding memory rather than raw compute.

The recommendation pod is the clearest case of memory placement, not compute, driving the layout, and it is the physical form of Archetype B (DLRM at Scale). Its embedding tables reach 10 TB or more, which lighthouse 2.1 showed would need at least 15 DGX H100 nodes of HBM merely to hold, while the dense neural-network layers that operate on those embeddings are comparatively small. A recommendation-first design splits the two across the memory hierarchy. The terabyte-scale embedding tables reside in host CPU DRAM (roughly $3–$5/GB), and the dense layers execute on GPUs, where the compute density justifies the $10–$15/GB cost of HBM. Embedding tables create a capacity requirement, but small random lookups can also saturate aggregate memory bandwidth or expose access latency at high concurrency. Host placement trades capacity cost against lookup throughput and host-to-accelerator transfer, so the workload must validate both budgets. The systems lesson is that optimal infrastructure is workload-specific, not specification-maximizing. Deploying compute-optimized nodes for a memory-capacity-bound workload would strand most of the accelerator’s throughput on a problem its silicon was never the bottleneck for.

The physical design supplies constraints, not an operating policy; later chapters turn its power, capacity, topology, reliability, and sustained-throughput measurements into placement, recovery, monitoring, and ownership decisions. One hardware decision remains for this chapter. With every level from the accelerator to the warehouse-scale computer in view, the choice of accelerator can now be judged against the whole hierarchy rather than against its data sheet.

↪ The ownership decision, in which sustained fleet utilization sets the build-versus-buy threshold, is developed in Utilization as the economic invariant.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Barroso and Hölzle’s Warehouse-Scale Computer (WSC) thesis reframes an ML pod from ‘a room full of computers’ to ‘a single computer that fills a room.’ Which practical consequence most directly shapes infrastructure engineering at pod scale?

    1. The entire facility is engineered as a unified machine where the network fabric acts as the system bus, distributed storage acts as the disk subsystem, and the cluster orchestrator acts as the operating system.
    2. All servers share a single hardware cache-coherence bus across the entire building, eliminating the need for message-passing software.
    3. Workloads are structured as embarrassingly parallel, uncoordinated batch jobs that completely isolate nodes from one another.
    4. Component failure rates decrease at warehouse scale, allowing fault tolerance to be managed as an offline manual process.

  2. A team doubles a distributed training cluster from 1,024 to 2,048 GPUs and observes wall-clock training time drop from 14 days to 10 days rather than the ideal 7 days. Calculate the realized scaling efficiency, determine whether the cost per useful FLOP increased, and identify the next diagnostic step.

  3. A synchronous training job spans 10,000 GPUs. Assume a hard crash is detected and recovered from a valid checkpoint, while a persistent gray failure remains undetected and delays dependent steps. Why can the gray failure cost more useful work over a long run?

    1. Gray failures permanently corrupt the model weights, forcing the entire training run to restart from step zero.
    2. Gray failures trip circuit breakers in the rack, causing cascading facility blackouts.
    3. The hard crash incurs detection, restart, and lost-work costs, while the undetected straggler can repeatedly delay the participating job until it is diagnosed.
    4. Gray failures disable FP8 Tensor Cores, silently falling back to double-precision FP64 and exhausting memory.

  4. A pod fabric that uses tiers of switches to supply full bisection bandwidth across node uplinks, in contrast to a direct-connect torus, is called a ____ topology.

  5. Arrange the stages in the worked scenario’s accounting sequence: begin with the compute-only baseline, add the distributed-step losses, then checkpointing, restart, and maintenance allowances. (1) Checkpoint I/O overhead (\(\approx 3\%\)), (2) Compute-only training time (\(6PD\) FLOPs divided by aggregate peak throughput times assumed local compute efficiency), (3) Hardware failure and restart overhead (\(\approx 10\%\)), (4) Scaling efficiency covering communication and pipeline bubbles (\(\approx 0.56\)), (5) Maintenance overhead (\(\approx 5\%\)). Each additive percentage is relative to the preceding subtotal.

2.8 Accelerator Selection for Fleet Workloads

The same H100 that sustains 30–50 percent of peak throughput during well-tuned large-batch training may achieve less than 5 percent utilization during single-request inference, because the binding constraint shifts from arithmetic to memory bandwidth. An accelerator’s compute throughput, memory bandwidth, memory capacity, and power consumption are meaningful only in relation to the workload running on it. Selecting the right accelerator therefore requires mapping each workload archetype to its position on the roofline plot and identifying which physical resource dominates its performance.

For the 175B running example, large local matrix operations can place substantial training work above the roofline ridge point. Arithmetic throughput then matters alongside the capacity needed for sharded state and the interconnect needed by the partition. H100, B200, and TPU v5p configurations are candidates to evaluate under the same workload and quality target. The selected software stack must express the required operations efficiently enough for the hardware advantage to appear in sustained execution.

Serving the same language model changes the answer, for the reason the token-latency notebook 2.1 established. At batch size 1, the H100’s 3.35 TB/s bandwidth determines decode throughput while much of the arithmetic silicon waits, so the key metric becomes memory bandwidth per dollar rather than peak TFLOP/s. Batching helps for the reason section 2.3.2 quantified, reusing each weight read across more requests, which is why serving systems work so hard to batch without violating tail-latency service-level objectives (SLOs). The workload did not change names, but the binding term of the single-machine iron law moved from arithmetic to data movement, and the accelerator choice changes with it.

Where recommendation models split the decision across sparse lookup and dense computation, the memory-placement case that the recommendation pod of section 2.7.1 already resolved, vision and diffusion workloads move back toward arithmetic throughput because convolutions, attention blocks, and denoising networks reuse data heavily. Diffusion inference makes this especially clear. A standard Stable Diffusion v1.5 configuration runs 50 denoising steps, so the service pays for a sequence of full model evaluations rather than one decode step per token. For these workloads, TFLOP/s per dollar often dominates bandwidth per dollar once model state fits in memory.

Mixture of experts (MoE) activates only part of a much larger parameter set for each token (Shazeer et al. 2017); Mixtral-8x7B (Jiang et al. 2024) has 46.7B total parameters but only 12.9B active per token because its router chooses 2 experts of 8 experts experts. That saving routes tokens among devices through the AllToAll collective, so the selection consequence is a fabric with enough bisection bandwidth, together with load balancing, for the theoretical saving to appear in the training trace (Fedus et al. 2022; Lepikhin et al. 2021). Multi-modal models similarly combine phases with different arithmetic intensity and batching behavior, such as vision encoding and text decode. Their relative cost and placement determine whether flexible scheduling, additional memory, or communication capacity changes the critical path; modality count alone does not select an accelerator architecture, so the planner must follow the data exchanged between the actual phases.

Shazeer, Noam, Azalia Mirhoseini, Krzysztof Maziarz, Andy Davis, Quoc Le, Geoffrey Hinton, and Jeff Dean. 2017. “Outrageously Large Neural Networks: The Sparsely-Gated Mixture-of-Experts Layer.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Jiang, A. Q., A. Sablayrolles, A. Roux, A. Mensch, B. Savary, C. Bamford, D. S. Chaplot, et al. 2024. “Mixtral of Experts.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2401.04088.
Fedus, William, Barret Zoph, and Noam Shazeer. 2022. “Switch Transformers: Scaling to Trillion Parameter Models with Simple and Efficient Sparsity.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 23 (120): 1–39.
Lepikhin, Dmitry, HyoukJoong Lee, Yuanzhong Xu, Dehao Chen, Orhan Firat, Yanping Huang, Maxim Krikun, Noam Shazeer, and Zhifeng Chen. 2021. “GShard: Scaling Giant Models with Conditional Computation and Automatic Sharding.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

↪ MoE AllToAll routing and expert load imbalance are developed in Dynamic routing and expert imbalance in MoE AllToAll.

Systems Perspective 2.2: Matching hardware to workload
Workload diversity can justify a heterogeneous fleet, with each pool provisioned for its binding resource. The gain comes from matching hardware to the work assigned to it, while keeping each tightly synchronized training group balanced. The fleet-wide decision must account for that matching benefit together with the cost of supporting multiple platforms.

2.8.1 Precision and throughput trade-offs

Booking a twofold training speedup from FP8 on a capacity spreadsheet, or sizing a serving pool for FP16 weights when INT4 would pass quality validation, can misstate fleet device counts by a factor of two or more. The number formats and peak rates of section 2.4 become fleet decisions as soon as they set how many accelerators a job requires. FP8 doubles the H100’s advertised arithmetic ceiling for supported operations, from 989 TFLOP/s to 1,979 TFLOP/s per GPU, but that doubling is a peak-capacity comparison, not a training speedup that can be booked before workload validation. Infrastructure sizing must use the sustained rate of a validated precision policy.

For inference, quantization changes both weight storage and the possible hardware topology. Our 175B model at FP16 requires 350 GB, so the parameters alone span at least five 80 GB H100 GPUs. Quantizing to INT8 halves the weight payload to 175 GB, which fits on three GPUs, and INT4 shrinks it further to 87.5 GB, which fits on two GPUs. These are weight-only capacity bounds; quantization metadata, attention state, and workspace also need memory. Reading 4-bit rather than 16-bit weights quarters the raw payload, but it does not change physical bandwidth or guarantee a fourfold token-latency reduction. Kernel support, conversion work, other traffic, and any change in device count determine the realized gain, subject to validation of model quality.

2.8.2 Benchmarking accelerator performance

A procurement team comparing accelerators needs a published number that predicts the performance the organization will actually achieve. Peak TFLOP/s, memory bandwidth, and TDP describe potential, not achieved performance, so the decision turns on whose measured results to trust and under what rules they were produced. The industry-standard reference is MLPerf, maintained by MLCommons: MLPerf Training reports the time to train reference models to a target accuracy, and MLPerf Inference reports serving throughput and latency under realistic batching and streaming conditions. Both capture complete system performance, including software stack efficiency, communication overhead, and memory management, rather than raw silicon speed alone.

Closed-division results fix the workload and quality target so implementations can be compared, and open-division results allow broader changes, rules that The measurement contract reads as one instance of a measurement contract. Neither division predicts a deployment automatically. Compare the submission’s workload, software, precision, hardware count, and quality target with the intended system before using its result for procurement.

For our 175B model, training, fine-tuning, and serving need separate measurements. Batch shape, trainable state, sequence length, and partitioning can change both capacity demand and the critical path. Fine-tuning does not occupy a fixed point between training and decode merely because it updates an existing model. Evaluate the intended phases on candidate hardware with the actual model and data pipeline, using published MLPerf results as comparative evidence rather than deployment forecasts. MFU reports the share of the chosen peak that useful model computation averages over a measurement window. Time to train a reference model to a target metric also captures the I/O stalls, checkpointing, and restart delays that a steady-state MFU window can omit, although neither establishes the model quality reached.

2.8.3 Inference-specific infrastructure considerations

Training and serving place opposite demands on the same cluster because they optimize over different timescales. A large training run is one long job that occupies its allocation for days or weeks and is judged by sustained throughput and time to a target quality. Its steps repeat the same exchanges, so communication demand is easier to characterize than an arrival stream, and an individual operation can take milliseconds to seconds without harm, although failures, I/O, and changes in execution shape still affect the schedule. Serving trained models must instead turn the same hardware into responses for many independent, short-lived requests arriving at irregular intervals, each under a latency target that for interactive use is measured in tens of milliseconds.

Serving infrastructure must meet latency targets at an acceptable cost per query under a variable request load. Independent requests can be assigned to separate replicas, but a replica that shards a model across devices still communicates within each request. Prompt processing, successive decode steps, and complete responses also have different latency budgets. Capacity planning must therefore specify the request mix, model placement, and service targets before concluding that serving needs less network capacity than training; training and serving part ways on utilization, model placement, network traffic, and the cost metric that judges them (table 16).

Serving therefore cannot be planned as training with smaller batches, because the optimization target changes from synchronized throughput to latency-bounded, continuously billed requests. The implication is that effective serving infrastructure often uses different hardware, different software, and different facility designs than the training infrastructure. Some organizations use previous-generation GPUs (A100s) for serving because the memory bandwidth per dollar can be competitive with newer GPUs, and the lower TDP (400 W vs. 700 W) allows higher rack density and lower cooling costs.

The inference workload itself splits into two distinct phases with opposing bottlenecks. The prefill phase processes prompt tokens in parallel, increasing weight reuse and potentially approaching the compute ceiling when the prompt and batch shapes supply enough work. The decode phase generates tokens sequentially for each request and is deeply memory-bandwidth-bound at small batch sizes. Serving systems raise utilization by admitting and retiring requests at iteration boundaries, called continuous batching, and by limiting how much prompt work delays the next decode iteration, mechanisms that Serving at Scale develops.

A napkin estimate shows how quickly serving capacity reaches training scale. One tensor-parallel replica of the running model on an eight-GPU node streams its FP16 weights once per decode step, which takes at least 13.1 ms at H100 HBM bandwidth. Batching 32 concurrent requests yields about 2,450 tokens/s per replica. A service handling 1,000 requests/s at 250 output tokens each must generate 250,000 tokens/s, which requires at least 103 replicas, or 824 GPUs, most of the 1,024-GPU training reference cluster. The estimate is a lower bound because it ignores key-value cache traffic, prefill work, and headroom for bursts and failures; Batching Strategies at Scale replaces it with batching models that account for key-value cache capacity, variable request lengths, and latency targets. Reliability objectives differ as well: a training job can absorb an occasional failure because checkpointing recovers the lost progress, whereas a stalled serving replica delays every request routed to it until traffic fails over.

2.8.4 The accelerator decision matrix

Combining workload regime, precision policy, and serving latency targets yields the procurement screen in table 17. Its candidate configurations rest on representative assumptions rather than fixed assignments of model families to bottlenecks or vendors: the rule is to diagnose the binding constraint first, then compare sustained useful work within the capacity, quality, latency, and facility budgets. The table’s constraint column is the per-device one, what a single accelerator must supply. The archetype roster of Three systems archetypes answers a different question, which constraint binds the fleet as a whole, so a workload can be compute-bound on each device and still communication-bound across the cluster, as Archetype A is.

For our 175B running example, table 17 separates the training purchase from the serving purchase. The workload constraint comes first; software support, deployment lifetime, and measured sustained throughput are compared only among candidates that clear it. A cheaper accelerator-hour is useful only if it supplies the required throughput within the capacity, latency, and facility budgets.

Checkpoint 2.4: Accelerator selection

An engineering team needs to deploy a 70B-parameter model for both training and inference. Training will use batch size 2048 across 256 GPUs for 3 months. Inference will serve 10,000 requests per second at batch size 1 for 2 years.

Each of these selections assumes the memory, communication, and power boundaries stay fixed at today’s package and rack limits. Hardware technologies now entering deployment do not remove those walls, but they relocate where each constraint binds across a facility’s lifetime.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team will serve a dense language model at batch size 1 with a strict per-token latency target. Which metric should dominate its comparison of candidate accelerators?

    1. Peak FP8 TFLOP/s per dollar, because decode performs two floating-point operations per parameter.
    2. Memory bandwidth per dollar, confirmed by measured throughput at the required latency, because batch-1 decode streams the weights with little reuse.
    3. Host DRAM capacity per dollar, because the weights should be served from host memory.
    4. Bisection bandwidth of the cluster fabric, because every request crosses the network.

  2. The 175B running model needs about 350 GB for FP16 weights. Using 80 GB accelerators, how many devices are needed for the weights alone at FP16, INT8, and INT4, and why does a fourfold smaller payload not guarantee a fourfold lower token latency?

  3. True or False: A workload whose binding constraint on each device is arithmetic throughput cannot be communication-bound across the cluster.

  4. MLPerf results that fix the workload and the quality target so that different implementations can be compared belong to the ____ division.

2.9 Infrastructure Technologies That Move the Walls

A new data center will host several generations of accelerators over its facility life, which operators typically depreciate across 15–20 years (Barroso et al. 2019), so future-facing technologies matter only when they change a durable planning constraint. The useful test is not whether a technology is novel, but which wall it moves, what new wall appears after the move, and which fleet-design decision changes as a result. Compute Express Link (CXL) moves the memory-capacity boundary, wafer-scale integration the chip-to-chip communication boundary, and optical circuit switching the fabric topology and power boundary, while advanced packaging moves the die boundary inside the accelerator package. None of them repeals the physics of data movement; each relocates the place where infrastructure teams must spend power, cooling, cost, and software complexity.

Barroso, Luiz André, Urs Hölzle, and Parthasarathy Ranganathan. 2019. The Datacenter as a Computer: Designing Warehouse-Scale Machines. Synthesis Lectures on Computer Architecture. Springer International Publishing. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-01761-2.

2.9.1 Compute express link (CXL)

Holding 1,400 GB of Adam optimizer state or terabyte-scale embedding tables inside accelerator HBM consumes the most expensive memory tier in the cluster for data touched only once per parameter update or sparse lookup. CXL addresses this capacity bottleneck by building protocols for I/O, caching, and coherent memory access on top of the PCIe physical layer. Supported device types and platform configurations determine which of those capabilities are available. Memory expanders and pooled memory can make host capacity less rigid, but accelerator access also requires a supported data path and software placement. Fleet planning must establish those capabilities before treating a pool as available training memory.

For ML training, the natural target is cold or phase-local state rather than the active matrix-multiply working set. The 1,400 GB Adam optimizer state of our 175B model is touched during parameter updates rather than streamed through every Tensor Core operation. A shared CXL pool could therefore reduce per-node memory pressure by holding optimizer shards, embedding-table spillover, or local data-cache buffers outside the accelerator HBM tier. The training framework still has to schedule this state carefully, but the placement problem becomes less binary than “fits in this node” or “spills to storage.”

The new wall is bandwidth. A representative CXL memory path over PCIe Gen5 x16 provides 64 GB/s, while H100 HBM provides 3.35 TB/s. That 52× gap means weights and activations for the forward and backward passes still belong in HBM. CXL is a capacity tier, not an HBM replacement. For fleet design, planners should ask whether server platforms, rack layouts, and orchestration software can absorb CXL memory pooling, not whether CXL removes the need for high-bandwidth accelerator memory.

HBM remains 52× faster, so CXL is capacity only.

2.9.2 Wafer-scale planning constraint

At the opposite extreme from CXL sits the wafer-scale engine introduced in section 2.2.1. Its on-wafer fabric removes package- and board-level transfers for mapped operations, but it does not make communication free. Infrastructure planning instead shifts toward capacity, placement, and injection bandwidth.

Our 175B running model needs 350 GB for FP16 weights alone, far beyond the 44 GB of on-wafer SRAM. Cerebras addresses this through weight streaming: external MemoryX units feed weights to the wafer over a 1.2 TB/s path while the wafer executes each layer, decoupling model capacity from the on-wafer memory. Weight streaming was designed for training. Activations stay on the wafer while weights stream in layer by layer and gradients stream back to MemoryX, which performs the optimizer update. The approach works when each layer’s computation is large enough to hide the streaming time. The wafer trades the communication wall for a streaming-bandwidth wall.

The lesson for infrastructure planners is not that wafer-scale systems replace GPU fleets. It is that memory proximity and memory capacity trade against each other. WSE-style designs buy extraordinary local bandwidth by putting the working set near the arithmetic, while CXL buys flexible capacity by moving memory farther away. HBM sits between those extremes: off-chip but on-package, smaller than host memory but fast enough for the active computation. The right architecture is the one whose memory tier matches the workload’s reuse pattern.

2.9.3 Reconfigurable topologies and optical circuit switching

Beyond the package, optical circuit switching (OCS) moves the fabric topology and power boundary. Micro-electro-mechanical systems (MEMS) mirror arrays steer light between fiber ports without converting it to electrical form. A pod’s physical topology can therefore be rewired for each job and around failed hardware, as in Google’s TPU v4 pods. Millisecond-scale reconfiguration, however, limits OCS to job-level allocation rather than per-message routing. Optical circuit switching and reconfigurable topology develops the mechanism, the TPU v4 deployment, and its fault-isolation limits.

The common theme is relocation rather than elimination. CXL pushes capacity outward into a fabric where PCIe bandwidth becomes the limiting discipline, whereas wafer-scale integration pulls communication inward onto silicon where on-wafer capacity and yield take over. In the same way, advanced packaging, with 2.5D interposers such as TSMC’s CoWoS and 3D stacking such as SoIC, compresses die-to-die distance past the reticle limit. It leaves the rack to absorb the resulting power density, which must be checked against the package and cooling budgets. Optical circuit switching, in turn, removes the optical-electrical-optical conversion inside the switch only to make reconfiguration latency the new boundary. Infrastructure planning therefore still begins by identifying where the next byte moves, how quickly it must arrive, and what physical system must be built so that movement does not dominate the workload.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A representative CXL memory path over PCIe Gen5 x16 delivers roughly \(64\text{ GB/s}\) of bandwidth, approximately \(50\times\) lower than an H100’s \(3.35\text{ TB/s}\) HBM3 bandwidth. Which infrastructure bottleneck is CXL memory pooling designed to relieve, and which bottleneck does it explicitly not solve?

    1. It relieves peak Tensor Core throughput shortages, but does not solve the memory capacity limit.
    2. It relieves rack cooling limits by eliminating electrical resistance, but does not solve chip-to-chip latency.
    3. It relieves the memory capacity ceiling by holding phase-local optimizer state in shared pooled memory, but explicitly does not replace HBM for bandwidth-critical forward and backward pass weight streaming.
    4. It relieves wafer-scale weight injection bottlenecks, but does not solve semiconductor yield.

  2. A systems team proposes moving a 1.8-trillion parameter LLM training workload to a Wafer-Scale Engine (such as Cerebras WSE-3) to eliminate chip-to-chip network communication delays. Explain why this move changes rather than eliminates communication constraints and identify the resulting capacity and bandwidth budgets.

  3. Advanced packaging technologies (such as 2.5D silicon interposers and 3D chiplet stacking) enable dense die-to-die interconnects on a single package. According to systems engineering principles, how does advanced packaging alter the landscape of infrastructure bottlenecks?

    1. It allows accelerators to run uncooled at ambient room temperature by eliminating electrical resistance across die boundaries.
    2. It permanently eliminates both the memory wall and the power wall by integrating optical waveguides directly onto the silicon substrate.
    3. It eliminates the need for distributed training frameworks because all model parameters can fit permanently in on-die SRAM caches.
    4. It shortens die-to-die paths while requiring package power density, current transients, and heat removal to be checked against the intended configuration.

  4. True or False: Emerging infrastructure technologies such as CXL memory pooling, wafer-scale integration, and 2.5D packaging eliminate physical infrastructure constraints, allowing data center operators to deploy dense ML clusters without modifying cooling or power delivery.

2.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Because the bottlenecks move rather than disappear, compute infrastructure teams often optimize the visible specification while missing the binding physical constraint. Each fallacy and pitfall below turns a node, rack, pod, or facility lesson into an operational warning.

Fallacy: More GPUs always means faster training.

Fallacies and Pitfalls warned against reading a compute budget as a wall-clock prediction. The hardware form of the same error assumes that training time scales linearly with GPU count, so that doubling the GPUs halves the training time. In practice, communication overhead grows with cluster size, and this chapter’s modeled scaling efficiencies show how fast.

Amdahl’s law bounds speedup when a fixed workload has a fixed serial fraction. Distributed training also incurs communication, pipeline, and I/O costs that can change with partitioning and scale; these are not all an invariant serial fraction. For a 175B-parameter model with 350 GB of gradients, the AllReduce at each training step must combine contributions across the participating group. It can do so through ring or tree exchanges; direct transfers between every pair are not required.

Even for a carefully engineered fabric, the modeled scaling efficiency of the running model falls from 0.87 at 256 GPUs to 0.83 at 1,024 GPUs and 0.56 at 8,192. Growing from the reference cluster to the 8,192-GPU scenario of section 2.7 therefore multiplies the hardware 8× but the speed only 5.4×, and 44 percent of the larger cluster’s locally achievable throughput is lost to communication and pipeline bubbles. That run still finishes in days, so the scale is worth buying for a deadline, but each added GPU returns less than the one before it; without an engineered fabric and overlapped communication, the curve flattens far sooner.

The correct approach is to profile useful compute time, exposed communication time, and idle time at the current scale before buying the next pod. If the added GPUs mostly increase exposed communication, procurement is buying idle time rather than training progress. The mathematics of scaling efficiency turns this local ratio into a scaling-efficiency estimate before committing to procurement.

Pitfall: Planning training throughput from peak FLOP/s alone.

Peak FLOP/s describes only one roofline ceiling. At the H100 FP16 ridge point of approximately 295.2 FLOP/byte, the bandwidth and arithmetic ceilings meet; lower-intensity operations cannot reach the arithmetic peak through more compute alone. In the chapter’s batch-1 weight-streaming estimate, decode near 1 FLOP/byte uses well under 1 percent of the arithmetic peak. Prefill and training supply more reuse, yet even well-tuned large training runs commonly sustain only 30–50 percent of peak once memory-bound attention and normalization kernels, activation recomputation, dependencies, and communication are counted. Procurement needs those measurements for the intended execution phase.

Fallacy: Power and cooling constraints can be handled after accelerator purchase.

Teams that plan GPU purchases from compute requirements without verifying that the target facility can deliver the required power and cooling run into hard physical limits with long lead times. A single rack of four DGX H100 systems requires 33.5 kW of power. Upgrading a data center’s electrical infrastructure (new transformers, switchgear, and UPS capacity) and its cooling plant (chillers, piping, pumps) are construction projects planned in months, not days. Even seemingly simple changes, such as upgrading the PDU in a single rack from 20 kW to 60 kW, can require new cabling and circuit breaker panels. Organizations that order hardware without confirming facility readiness risk having millions of dollars of GPUs sitting in shipping containers in the parking lot while the building is retrofitted. Accelerators can arrive faster than a facility can be upgraded to power and cool them, which makes this mismatch a practical planning risk.

Pitfall: Buying a newer accelerator without checking the workload’s arithmetic intensity.

Suppose a team deploys B200 GPUs (4,500 TFLOP/s FP8 peak) for an inference workload serving a 7B-parameter model at batch size 1, where an arithmetic intensity of approximately 1 FLOP/byte places the workload deep in the memory-bound region. The B200’s memory bandwidth (8 TB/s) is 2.4× higher than the H100’s (3.35 TB/s). Comparing B200 FP8 against H100 FP16/BF16-class throughput gives a peak arithmetic ratio of about 4.6×, but also changes the precision convention. At a fixed weight footprint, the bandwidth-only model instead predicts a gain of about 2.4×. The low intensity limits the fraction of arithmetic peak that this model can use, though it does not measure idle time; the comparison that matters is bandwidth per dollar at the measured serving rate, and the larger arithmetic peak alone does not establish better value.

Fallacy: Air cooling is sufficient for AI workloads.

Operators of legacy facilities may assume that adding fans and chiller capacity is sufficient for any denser AI rack. In practice, the cooling choice depends on the system’s heat-rejection design and the facility’s available airflow, pressure, inlet temperature, and containment. Engineered air remains viable for some dense configurations, while higher rack densities can require direct liquid cooling to avoid thermal throttling or exceeding facility limits.

Fallacy: All GPU-hours are equivalent.

A common error in cost comparisons is to treat one GPU-hour on an H100 as interchangeable with one GPU-hour on an A100 or V100. Under the same dense FP16 peak convention, H100 offers 3.2× the arithmetic rate of A100 and 7.9× that of V100. If a workload sustained the same fraction of those peaks with the same operation count and target quality, 10,000 V100 GPU-hours would correspond to approximately 1,264 H100 GPU-hours. That is a conditional compute-only comparison, not a predicted training time, because memory, communication, numerical recipes, and software can change sustained efficiency. Procurement must compare measured cost per useful computation or per completed run, rather than rental price or peak FLOP/s alone.

Pitfall: Upgrading the network before identifying the training bottleneck.

A network upgrade from 200 Gb/s to 400 Gb/s InfiniBand changes training time only through the communication it accelerates on the critical path, the mirror image of the faster-accelerator fallacy in Fallacies and Pitfalls. In a no-overlap example with 20 seconds of computation and 2 seconds of communication, doubling transfer bandwidth reduces the estimated step from 22 to 21 seconds, an improvement of 4.5 percent. That result assumes the communication term scales with bandwidth and other terms remain fixed. Adding GPUs or optimizing kernels requires a separate estimate because either can change communication and overlap. The Fleet Law keeps those costs visible when comparing investments.

Fallacy: Average power draw is sufficient for cooling design.

Accelerator power draw varies across communication, memory, and compute phases. A workload average below the configured sustained envelope does not establish the cooling requirement because synchronized compute phases can raise many devices toward that envelope together. Thermal design must use the vendor’s sustained design target under the intended inlet and cooling conditions, while the electrical path must separately tolerate documented transients. Otherwise thermal throttling can reduce sustained throughput without producing a crash or explicit application error.

Pitfall: Selecting accelerators by HBM capacity without checking bandwidth.

Capacity and bandwidth answer separate feasibility questions, and a model that fits in HBM but cannot be served within its latency target is as unusable as one that does not fit. Consider two hypothetical options for a 70-billion-parameter model: (a) 192 GB of HBM2e at 2.04 TB/s, and (b) 80 GB of HBM3 at 3.35 TB/s. Option (a) holds the 141 GB FP16 weight payload, whose streaming floor is 141 GB/2.04 TB/s = 69.2 ms. Option (b) cannot hold 141 GB in its 80 GB capacity, but the 71 GB INT8 payload fits before other live state is counted. Its weight-transfer floor is 71 GB/3.35 TB/s = 21 ms, a 3.3× ratio between the two estimates. That ratio combines faster memory with fewer bytes; option (a) could also use a validated INT8 recipe. Selection must compare like-for-like quality, complete live-state capacity, and measured latency at the intended concurrency.

Fallacy: Data loading can be fixed after deployment.

Teams that optimize only GPU kernels and communication can still find their accelerators waiting for input. The data loading pipeline must sustain the cluster’s consumption rate across storage, preprocessing, and host-to-GPU transfer. The chapter’s pre-tokenized language-model example needs modest raw bandwidth, but tokenization, sequence packing, and shuffling can change the host-side demand. Size CPU workers, buffering, and storage concurrency from the actual pipeline. Local NVMe staging and prefetching are useful when they relieve the measured bottleneck; neither substitutes for testing the complete path at fleet concurrency.

Pitfall: Buying homogeneous clusters without modeling workload diversity.

Uniform hardware simplifies support and placement, but that benefit must be weighed against the useful work existing devices can still perform. For our 175B model, training and low-batch decode stress different resources. Separate pools can exploit that difference without placing mismatched devices in the same tightly synchronized group.

The A100’s 2.04 TB/s and the H100’s 3.35 TB/s supply different bandwidth ceilings. Retaining older devices for an inference pool can reduce cost when their measured throughput, power, and support costs compare favorably at the required latency. The distinction is between job-level balance within a synchronized group and cluster-level hardware uniformity. A heterogeneous fleet can preserve balanced groups, but its matching benefit must cover the added scheduling and operational complexity.

Pitfall: Treating commissioning and burn-in as schedule slack.

Commissioning is part of the system build, not a buffer that can be consumed when hardware delivery slips. Burn-in tests, cable validation, firmware qualification, thermal soak, and NCCL stress runs are the first time the node, rack, network, and facility layers operate as one machine. Compressing that phase turns installation defects into production failures, where each debugging cycle idles the full fleet rather than a small acceptance-test window.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A 175B-parameter model training run on 1,024 GPUs has a step time of \(1{,}000\text{ ms}\), broken down into \(700\text{ ms}\) of compute (\(T_{\text{compute}}\)) and \(300\text{ ms}\) of gradient AllReduce communication (\(T_{\text{comm}}\)). If the team scales out to 2,048 GPUs (assuming compute scales linearly to \(350\text{ ms}\), while AllReduce communication time remains constant at \(300\text{ ms}\)), what is the resulting scaling efficiency?

    1. 100% (ideal linear speedup, halving step time to \(500\text{ ms}\)).
    2. \(\approx 77\%\) (speedup of \(\approx 1.54\times\) from \(1{,}000\text{ ms}\) to \(650\text{ ms}\), demonstrating diminishing returns on the \(2\times\) hardware investment).
    3. 50% (step time remains completely unchanged at \(1{,}000\text{ ms}\)).
    4. \(\approx 125\%\) (superlinear speedup due to increased aggregate L2 cache capacity).

  2. Explain how neglecting facility readiness and commissioning can delay an ML training program by months even after GPUs have been delivered to the loading dock. Identify two concrete critical-path failure modes.

  3. A team serving a 70B-parameter LLM at batch size 1 on H100s observes poor token generation latency and proposes upgrading to a B200 (\(4{,}500\text{ FP8 TFLOP/s}\) peak, \(\approx 8\text{ TB/s}\) HBM3e) primarily to capture the \(\approx 2.3\times\) boost in peak arithmetic throughput. Which hidden assumption in their reasoning is fundamentally flawed?

    1. Assuming liquid-cooled racks are capable of hosting high-density B200 servers.
    2. Assuming checkpoint storage bandwidth is necessary during inference serving.
    3. Assuming InfiniBand switches are required for single-node inference deployments.
    4. Assuming that peak arithmetic throughput governs performance when the modeled batch-1 decode workload is limited by weight bandwidth; the HBM bandwidth ratio is the relevant first estimate.

  4. A cost analyst evaluates cloud GPU pricing by comparing hourly rates: H100 at $4.00/GPU-hour vs. V100 at $0.80/GPU-hour, concluding that V100 is \(5\times\) cheaper. Using throughput equivalence (an H100 delivers \(\approx 7.9\times\) the FP16 Tensor throughput of a V100), explain why this hourly rate comparison is a procurement fallacy.
